Fourier Transforms of Irregular Holonomic D-modules, Singularities at Infinity of Meromorphic Functions and Irregular Characteristic Cycles 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification: 14C17, 14D06, 32C38, 32S60, 35A27
Kiyoshi TAKEUCHI

Abstract
Based on the recent developments in the irregular Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for holonomic D-modules and the Fourier-Sato transforms for enhanced ind-sheaves, we study the Fourier transforms of some irregular holonomic D-modules. For this purpose, the singularities of rational and meromorphic functions on complex affine varieties will be studied precisely, with the help of some new methods and tools such as meromorphic vanishing cycle functors. As a consequence, we show that the exponential factors and the irregularities of the Fourier transform of a holonomic D-module are described geometrically by the stationary phase method, as in the classical case of dimension one. A new feature in the higher-dimensional case is that we have some extra rank jump of the Fourier transform produced by the singularities of the linear perturbations of the exponential factors at their points of indeterminacy. In the course of our study, not necessarily homogeneous Lagrangian cycles that we call irregular characteristic cycles will play a crucial role.



1
Introduction

The theory of Fourier transforms of
D-modules is one of the most active areas in algebraic analysis.
They interchange algebraic holonomic D-modules on the complex
vector spaces
\mathbb{C}^{N}
with those on their duals.
Until now, the case
N=1
has been studied precisely by many
mathematicians such as Bloch-Esnault
[
4
]
,
Malgrange
[
42
]
, Mochizuki

[
47
]
, Sabbah
[
63
]
etc.
On the other hand, after a groundbreaking
development in the theory of irregular meromorphic
connections by Kedlaya
[
35
,
36
]
and Mochizuki

[
48
]
, in
[
9
]
D’Agnolo and Kashiwara
established the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for
irregular holonomic D-modules
(for another Riemann-Hilbert correspondence
via filtered local
systems, see also Sabbah
[
64
]
).
For this purpose,
they introduced enhanced ind-sheaves extending the
classical notion of ind-sheaves introduced by
Kashiwara-Schapira
[
29
]
.
Moreover in
[
32
]
,
Kashiwara and Schapira adapted this new notion to
the Fourier-Sato transforms of Tamarkin
[
67
]

and developed a new theory of Fourier-Sato transforms
for enhanced ind-sheaves which correspond to those
for algebraic holonomic D-modules. Subsequently
in
[
10
]
, by using
these results, D’Agnolo and Kashiwara
studied Fourier transforms of
holonomic D-modules on the affine line
\mathbb{C}
very precisely.
In this case
N=1
, later
Fourier transforms of regular and irregular holonomic
D-modules were studied from various points of view by
many authors such as D’Agnolo-Hien-Morando-Sabbah

[
8
]
, Hohl
[
20
]
,
Mochizuki
[
49
]
and
Barco-Hien-Hohl-Sevenheck
[
2
]
etc.
However, in contrast to these achievements in
N=1
,
we know only very little in the
higher-dimensional case
N\geq 2
.
The aim of this paper is to clarify
this situation by extending our previous
results for Fourier transforms of
regular holonomic D-modules in

[
22
]
and
[
23
]
to more general
holonomic D-modules.
For this purpose, we will study the singularities
of rational and meromorphic functions on complex
affine varieties precisely,
by using some new methods and tools such as meromorphic
vanishing cycle functors.

First, let us briefly recall the definition of
Fourier transforms of algebraic D-modules.
Let
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}^{N}
be a complex vector space
and
Y=\mathbb{C}_{w}^{N}
its dual.
We regard them as algebraic varieties and
use the notations
\mathcal{D}_{X}
and
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
for
the sheaves of the
rings of “algebraic” differential operators on them.
Denote by
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
(resp.
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
)
the category of coherent (resp. holonomic)
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.
Let
W_{N}:=\mathbb{C}[z,\partial_{z}]\simeq\Gamma(X;\mathcal{D}_{X})
and

W^{\ast}_{N}:=\mathbb{C}[w,\partial_{w}]\simeq\Gamma(Y;\mathcal{D}_{Y})

be the Weyl algebras over
X
and
Y
, respectively.
Then there exists a ring isomorphism

W_{N}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}W^{\ast}_{N}\hskip 30.0pt(z_{i}\mapsto-\partial_{w_{i}},\ \partial_{z_{i}}\mapsto w_{i}),

(1.1)

by which we can endow a left
W_{N}
-module
M
with
a structure of a left
W_{N}^{\ast}
-module.
We call it the Fourier transform of
M

and denote it by
M^{\wedge}
.
For a ring
R
we denote by
\mathrm{Mod}_{f}(R)

the category of finitely generated
R
-modules.
Recall that for the affine algebraic varieties
X

and
Y
we have the equivalences of categories

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Mod}_{f}(\Gamma(X;\mathcal{D}_{X}))=\mathrm{Mod}_{f}(W_{N}),

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Mod}_{f}(\Gamma(Y;\mathcal{D}_{Y}))=\mathrm{Mod}_{f}(W^{\ast}_{N})

(1.3)

obtained by taking global sections
(see e.g.
[
21
, Propositions 1.4.4 and 1.4.13]
).
Thus, for a coherent
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

we can define its Fourier
transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})
.
It follows that we obtain an equivalence of categories

(\cdot)^{\wedge}:\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})

(1.4)

between the subcategories of holonomic D-modules
(see e.g.
[
21
, Proposition 3.2.7]
for the details).
Although the definition of Fourier transforms of
holonomic D-modules is so simple, in general it is
hard to describe their properties.
First of all, the Fourier transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
of a regular
holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{M}
is
not necessarily regular. For the regularity of

\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
we need some very strong condition on
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
X^{{\rm an}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}
be the underlying complex
manifold of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
that we sometimes denote by
X

for simplicity.
Recall that an algebraic constructible sheaf

\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}}):={\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{X^{{\rm an}}})
on
X^{{\rm an}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}

is called monodromic if
its cohomology sheaves are locally constant
on each
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-orbit in
X^{{\rm an}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}
(see
Verdier
[
70
]
).
Then the following beautiful theorem
is due to Brylinski
[
6
]
.

Theorem 1.1

((Brylinski
[
6
]
))
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an algebraic regular holonomic
D-module on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Assume that its
solution complex
Sol_{X}(\mathcal{M})
is
monodromic. Then its
Fourier transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
is regular
and
Sol_{Y}(\mathcal{M}^{\wedge})
is monodromic.

In
[
22
]
and
[
23
]
, removing the
monodromicity assumption in this theorem,
the author and Ito studied the Fourier transforms of general
regular holonomic D-modules
\mathcal{M}
on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
described their smooth loci, exponential factors, irregularities and
characteristic cycles etc. in terms of the geometry
of
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, as was clarified in

[
23
]
, if
\mathcal{M}
is regular holonomic then

Sol_{Y}(\mathcal{M}^{\wedge})
is monodromic.
For the other important contributions in the
regular case, see also Daia
[
11
]
.
In this paper, removing also
the regularity assumption
in
[
11
]
,
[
22
]
and
[
23
]
,
we study the Fourier transforms of more general
holonomic D-modules
\mathcal{M}
.
Namely, we aim at finding a way to get a unified
generalization of the results in
[
10
]

and
[
22
]
.

Now let us explain our mains results
more precisely. Mostly
in this paper, we consider the following special but basic
holonomic D-modules. For a rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
P,Q\in\Gamma(X;\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{N}]
,

Q\not=0
) on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
we set

U:=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
and
define an
exponential
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{U|X}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
as in the analytic case
(see Subsection
4.5
). In what
follows, we always assume that
P
and
Q
are
coprime. Then
I(f):=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

is noting but the set of the points of
indeterminacy of the rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}
.

Definition 1.2
.

We say that a holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module

\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
is an exponentially twisted holonomic
D-module if there exist a regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and a rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
P,Q\in\Gamma(X;\mathcal{O}_{X}),Q\not=0
) on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
such that we have an isomorphism

\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathcal{N}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{E}^{f}_{U|X},

(1.5)

where
for the hypersurface
D:=Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
we have

\bigl(\mathcal{N}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{E}^{f}_{U|X}\bigr)(*D)\simeq\mathcal{N}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{E}^{f}_{U|X}
and hence the right
hand side is concentrated in degree
0
.

Exponentially twisted holonomic
D-modules can be considered as
natural prototypes or building blocks
for general holonomic D-modules, in view of
the recent progress in
[
35
,
36
]
,

[
48
]
and
[
9
]
.
Since for the moment there is no efficient
way to describe the enhanced solution complexes
of general holonomic D-modules in higher
dimensions
N\geq 2
, exponentially twisted holonomic
D-modules are the most typical
holonomic D-modules to which the
general theory of
[
32
]
is
applicable. From now on, we fix an
exponentially twisted holonomic
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module

\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
such that

\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathcal{N}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{E}^{f}_{U|X}

for a regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and a rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
on
X
and explain
our results on its
Fourier transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
.
Let us call
f
the exponential factor of
\mathcal{M}
. We set

K:=Sol_{X}(\mathcal{N})\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}}).

(1.6)

Here we use the convention that
its shift
K[N]\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})

is a perverse sheaf on
X^{{\rm an}}
and
denote the support of
K\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})

by
Z\subset X
. Let
\pi:X^{{\rm an}}\times\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow X^{{\rm an}}
be the projection.
Then for the enhanced sheaf

F:=\pi^{-1}K\otimes{\rm E}_{U^{{\rm an}}|X^{{\rm an}}}^{{\rm Re}f}=\pi^{-1}K\otimes\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\{(z,t)\in X\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ z\in U,t+{\rm Re}f(z)\geq 0\}}\Big)\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{+}(\mathbb{C}_{X^{{\rm an}}})

(1.7)

on the underlying real analytic manifold
X_{\mathbb{R}}=X^{{\rm an}}
of
X
, we have an isomorphism

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\rm E}_{X^{{\rm an}}}\overset{+}{\otimes}F\simeq\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \pi^{-1}K\otimes\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\{(z,t)\in X\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ z\in U,t+{\rm Re}f(z)\geq a\}}\Big)

(1.8)

of enhanced ind-sheaves on
X_{\mathbb{R}}

(see Section
4
). We define
its enhanced micro-support
{\rm SS}^{{\rm E}}(F)\subset(T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}})\times\mathbb{R}
and the reduced one

{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)\subset T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}
as in D’Agnolo-Kashiwara
[
10
]

and Tamarkin
[
67
]
.
In this paper, we call
{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)

the irregular micro-support of
F
.
Note that for the (not necessarily homogeneous)
complex Lagrangian submanifold

\Lambda^{f}:=\{(z,df(z))\ |\ z\in U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\}\subset T^{*}U

(1.9)

of
T^{*}U
via the natural identification
(T^{*}U)_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq T^{*}U_{\mathbb{R}}
we have

{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)\cap T^{*}U_{\mathbb{R}}=\bigl({\rm SS}(K)\cap T^{*}U_{\mathbb{R}}\bigr)+\Lambda^{f}.

(1.10)

Moreover we can show that
{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)

is contained in a complex isotropic analytic
subset of
(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}

(see Lemma
5.4
).
However, for the moment it is not clear for us if

{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)
itself
is a complex Lagrangian analytic subset or not.
This prevents us from applying the theory of
Fourier-Sato transforms for enhanced ind-sheaves
developed by Kashiwara-Schapira
[
32
]

especially when
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)\not=\emptyset
.
To overcome this difficulty, we define a (not necessarily
homogeneous) complex Lagrangian analytic
subset
{\rm SS}^{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{F})

of
(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}

in a different way
as follows and use it instead of
{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)
.
First, as a complex analogue of the

\mathbb{R}
-constructible sheaf

F=\pi^{-1}K\otimes{\rm E}_{U^{{\rm an}}|X^{{\rm an}}}^{{\rm Re}f}

on
X_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}
, by
the (not necessarily) closed embedding

i_{-f}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C},\qquad(z\longmapsto(z,-f(z)))

(1.11)

associated to the rational function

-f:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
we set

\mathcal{F}:=(i_{-f})_{!}(K|_{U})\quad\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C}).

(1.12)

Then we can easily show that
it is a constructible sheaf
on
X\times\mathbb{C}
and hence its micro-support

{\rm SS}(\mathcal{F})

is a homogeneous complex Lagrangian
analytic subset of
T^{*}(X\times\mathbb{C})
.
Then as in the definitions of
{\rm SS}^{{\rm E}}(F)\subset(T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}})\times\mathbb{R}

and
{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}(F)\subset T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}
,
by forgetting the homogeneity of
{\rm SS}(\mathcal{F})

we define the following subsets:

{\rm SS}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{F})\subset T^{*}X\times\mathbb{C},\qquad{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{F})\subset T^{*}X.

(1.13)

See Section
5
for the details.
It is clear that
{\rm SS}^{{\rm E},\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{F})

is a complex analytic subset of
(T^{*}X)\times\mathbb{C}
.
Let us call it the enhanced micro-support of

\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})
. We can also show that

{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{F})
is a
(not necessarily
homogeneous) complex Lagrangian analytic
subset of
(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}
and call it
the irregular micro-support of
\mathcal{F}
.
As a byproduct of the proof, we obtain also a
(not necessarily
homogeneous) Lagrangian cycle in

T^{*}X\simeq X\times Y
supported by

{\rm SS}_{{\rm irr}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{F})\subset T^{*}X

(see the proof of Lemma
5.6
).
As we can show that it depends only on
\mathcal{M}
,
we call it the irregular characteristic
cycle of
\mathcal{M}
and denote it by
{\rm CC}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{M})
.
For a point
w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
we define a
rational function
f^{w}
on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
by

f^{w}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\qquad(z\longmapsto\langle z,w\rangle-f(z)),

(1.14)

where for
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\in X
and

w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{N})\in Y
we set

\langle z,w\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}w_{i}\qquad\in\mathbb{C}.

(1.15)

Let

X\overset{p}{\longleftarrow}X\times Y\overset{q}{\longrightarrow}Y

(1.16)

be the projections.
Then by using our meromorphic vanishing cycle functors

\phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(\cdot):{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})\longrightarrow{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
(
c\in\mathbb{C}
) with compact support
(see Section
2
for their definition
and basic properties),
we define the following subset of

(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Definition 1.3
.

For the enhanced sheaf
F=\pi^{-1}K\otimes{\rm E}_{U^{{\rm an}}|X^{{\rm an}}}^{{\rm Re}f}\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{+}(\mathbb{C}_{X^{{\rm an}}})
we define a subset

{\rm SS}_{{\rm eva}}(F)\subset T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}
of
T^{*}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}
by

{\rm SS}_{{\rm eva}}(F):=\{(z,w)\in(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ \phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{z}\not=0\ \text{for some}\ c\in\mathbb{C}\}.

(1.17)

Note that we can calculate the stalks of
the meromorphic vanishing cycles
and hence
{\rm SS}_{{\rm eva}}(F)

by using resolutions of singularities of

f
and the results in

[
43
, Section 5]
(see
the final part of Section
2
).
We then will show that

{\rm SS}_{{\rm eva}}(F)
is contained in

\Lambda:={\rm SS}^{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{F})

and there exists a non-empty Zariski open
subset
\Omega\subset Y
of
Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}

such that the restriction of the projection

q:T^{*}X\simeq X\times Y\simeq T^{*}Y\longrightarrow Y

to
\Lambda={\rm SS}^{\mathbb{C}}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{F})\subset T^{*}X

is an unramified finite covering over

\Omega\subset Y
and satisfies the following properties.
For the precise construction of

\Omega
see Lemma
6.3
.
Let
V\subset\Omega^{{\rm an}}
be
a contractible open subset
of
\Omega^{{\rm an}}
so that for the decomposition

q^{-1}(V)\cap\Lambda=\Lambda_{V,1}\sqcup\Lambda_{V,2}\sqcup\cdots\cdots\sqcup\Lambda_{V,d}

(1.18)

of
q^{-1}(V)\cap\Lambda
into its connected components

\Lambda_{V,i}
(
1\leq i\leq d
) we have

\Lambda_{V,i}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V
for any
1\leq i\leq d
.
Then by our choice of
\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
,

Z_{i}:=p(\Lambda_{V,i})\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}

(
1\leq i\leq d
) are complex submanifolds of

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
and we can renumber them
so that for some
1\leq r\leq d
we have

Z_{i}\subset U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
(resp.

Z_{i}\subset I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)
) if
1\leq i\leq r

(resp. if
r+1\leq i\leq d
). Let

g_{i}:V\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
1\leq i\leq d
)
be holomorphic functions on

V\subset\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that

\chi(\Lambda_{V,i})=\Lambda^{g_{i}}:=\{(w,dg_{i}(w))\ |\ w\in V\}\subset T^{*}Y,

(1.19)

where we used the symplectic transformation
of
[
10
]
:

\chi:T^{*}X\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}Y\qquad((z,w)\longmapsto(w,-z)).

(1.20)

Note that such
g_{i}:V\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
are
uniquely defined modulo constant functions
on
V
. For
1\leq i\leq d
and
w\in V
let

\zeta^{(i)}(w)\in X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
be the
unique point of
Z_{i}\subset X
such that

(\zeta^{(i)}(w),w)\in\Lambda_{V,i}\subset\Lambda
so that we have

\Lambda_{V,i}=\{(\zeta^{(i)}(w),w)\ |\ w\in V\}\subset T^{*}Y\simeq T^{*}X.

(1.21)

Then by (
1.10
)
for any
1\leq i\leq r
and
w\in V
the point

(\zeta^{(i)}(w),df^{w}(\zeta^{(i)}(w)))=(\zeta^{(i)}(w),w-df(\zeta^{(i)}(w)))\in(T^{*}X)_{\mathbb{R}}

(1.22)

is contained in the smooth part of
{\rm SS}(K|_{U})\subset(T^{*}U)_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We denote by
m(i)\geq 1
the multiplicity of
the regular holonomic

\mathcal{D}_{X}
-module
\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
(or
of the perverse sheaf
K[N]
) there.
In fact, for any such
i
we have an equality

g_{i}(w)\equiv f(\zeta^{(i)}(w))-\langle\zeta^{(i)}(w),w\rangle=-f^{w}(\zeta^{(i)}(w))\qquad(w\in V)

(1.23)

modulo constant functions on
V
and can define

g_{i}:V\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

g_{i}(w):=-f^{w}(\zeta^{(i)}(w))\qquad(w\in V).

(1.24)

Also for
r+1\leq i\leq d
and
w\in V
, we can show that
there exist only finitely many
c\in\mathbb{C}

such that

\phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{\zeta^{(i)}(w)}\not=0

(1.25)

and for any such
c\in\mathbb{C}
we have a concentration

H^{j}\phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{\zeta^{(i)}(w)}\simeq 0\qquad(j\not=N-1).

(1.26)

Moreover, by our choice of
\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}

the dimension of the only non-trivial
cohomology group

H^{N-1}\phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{\zeta^{(i)}(w)}\not=0

is constant with respect to
w\in V\subset\Omega
.
For
r+1\leq i\leq d
we thus can set

m(i):=\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathbb{C}}{\rm dim}H^{N-1}\phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{\zeta^{(i)}(w)}\geq 1.

(1.27)

Namely for
r+1\leq i\leq d

the multiplicity
m(i)\geq 1
is defined
by the singularities of the rational functions

f^{w}
(
w\in V
) at their points of
indeterminacy
\zeta^{(i)}(w)\in I(f^{w})=I(f)=P^{-1}\cap Q^{-1}(0)
. In fact, for any such
i

we can also show that there exist (distinct) constants

a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
we have

\{c\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ \phi^{{\rm mero,c}}_{f^{w}-c}(K)_{\zeta^{(i)}(w)}\not=0\}=\{a_{1}-g_{i}(w),a_{2}-g_{i}(w),\ldots,a_{n_{i}}-g_{i}(w)\}

(1.28)

for any
w\in V
.

Let
i_{Y}:Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}\xhookrightarrow{\ \ \ }\overline{Y}={\mathbb{P}}^{N}

be the projective compactification of
Y
.
We extend the Fourier transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})

to the holonomic D-module

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}:=i_{Y\ast}(\mathcal{M}^{\wedge})\simeq\mathbf{D}i_{Y\ast}(\mathcal{M}^{\wedge})

on
\overline{Y}
.
Let
\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}
be the underlying complex manifold
of
\overline{Y}
and
define the analytification

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}^{{\rm an}}\in\mathrm{Mod}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}})

of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}
by

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}^{{\rm an}}:=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\overline{Y}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}
.
Then we have the following
formula for the enhanced solution complex

Sol_{\overline{Y}}^{\rm E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}):=Sol_{\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}}^{\rm E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}^{{\rm an}})\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}})

(1.29)

of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}}^{{\rm an}}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

In the situation as above, we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{V}\otimes\Big(Sol_{\overline{Y}}^{\rm E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}})\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{V^{{\rm an}}|\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}}^{{\rm Re}g_{i}}\bigr)^{\oplus m(i)}

\displaystyle\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\Big(\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{(w,t)\in\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ w\in V,\ t+{\rm Re}g_{i}(w)\geq a\}}\Big)^{\oplus m(i)}

of enhanced ind-sheaves on
\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}
.
In particular, the restriction

\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}|_{\Omega}
of the Fourier
transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
to
\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is an
algebraic integrable connection of rank

\sum_{i=1}^{d}m(i)
.

By Theorem
1.4
we can describe the
exponential factors and the irregularities
of the Fourier transform
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
of
\mathcal{M}

along various submanifolds of
Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
See Section
6
for the details.
By patching the
(not necessarily homogeneous) Lagrangian cycles

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}m(i)\cdot\bigl[\Lambda_{V,i}\bigr]

(1.30)

in the open subsets
q^{-1}(V)=X\times V\subset X\times Y\simeq T^{*}X
for various

V\subset\Omega
we obtain a Lagrangian cycle
globally defined in

q^{-1}(\Omega)=X\times\Omega\subset T^{*}X
. By our definitions of the multiplicities
m(i)\geq 1
,
it turns out that it coincides with the restriction of
the irregular characteristic
cycle
{\rm CC}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{M})
of
\mathcal{M}

to
q^{-1}(\Omega)=X\times\Omega\subset T^{*}X
.
Then the last assertion of Theorem
1.4
means that
the generic rank of the holonomic D-module

\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
is equal to the covering degree of

{\rm CC}_{{\rm irr}}(\mathcal{M})
over

\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. We thus
find that if
I(f)=P^{-1}\cap Q^{-1}(0)\not=\emptyset
there may exist some extra
rank jump of
\mathcal{M}^{\wedge}
produced by
the singularities of the rational functions

f^{w}
(
w\in\Omega
) i.e. the linear perturbations
of the exponential factor
f
of

\mathcal{M}
. This shows that the structures of
the Fourier transforms of irregular
holonomic D-modules are much more involved
than those of the regular ones studied in

[
6
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
22
]

and
[
23
]
etc.

Our proof of Theorem
1.4
is
similar to that of
[
22
, Theorem 4.4]
and
relies on a Morse theory for the Morse functions

{\rm Re}f^{w}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
(
w\in\Omega
) and

K|_{U}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(U^{{\rm an}})
as well as
the theory of Fourier-Sato transforms
for enhanced ind-sheaves of
Kashiwara-Schapira
[
32
]
.
However, it requires also
more careful analysis on the singularities
of the rational functions

f^{w}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
w\in\Omega
)
at infinity and at their points of
indeterminacy. To treat their singularities at
infinity, we extend the classical notion of
tameness at infinity introduced by
Broughton
[
5
]
for polynomial
functions on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
to that for
rational and meromorphic functions on
smooth subvarieties of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
obtain a transversality theorem (see
Proposition
3.4
) similar to
the ones proved by Némethi and Zaharia

[
52
]
,
[
53
]

(see also
[
56
, Sections 2 and 3]
for
related results).
Let
\mathcal{S}
be an algebraic Whitney stratification of
Z\cap U\subset U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
adapted to

K|_{U}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(U)
such that

{\rm SS}(K|_{U})\subset\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}T^{*}_{S}X

(1.31)

Then in Lemma
6.16
we will show that
for any
w\in\Omega
and stratum

S\in\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{S}
such that
T^{*}_{S}X\subset{\rm SS}(K|_{U})
the restriction

f^{w}|_{S}:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

of
f^{w}
to
S\subset U
is tame
at infinity in our (generalized) sense.
This implies that the morphisms

f^{w}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
w\in\Omega
)
and
K|_{U}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(U^{{\rm an}})

satisfy one of the conditions of Kashiwara and Schapira
in their non-proper direct image
theorem
[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]

for the family of open subsets

U_{r}\subset U
(
r>0
) of
U
defined by

U_{r}:=\{z\in U\ |\ ||z||<r\}\subset U\qquad(r>0)

(1.32)

(see Proposition
3.5
). We
then show that for the given rational function

f:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
we can apply

[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
to its
generic linear perturbations

\tilde{f}=-f^{w}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

(
w\in\Omega
) and
K|_{U}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(U^{{\rm an}})

to obtain the following byproduct
of our study of Fourier transforms
(see Proposition
3.10
and Lemma
6.17
).

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
f:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and

K\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
be as above.
Then for generic linear perturbations

\tilde{f}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
of
f
we have:
For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}

there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that for the
inclusion maps
i_{r}:U_{r}\hookrightarrow U

(
r>R
) we have isomorphisms

{\rm R}\tilde{f}_{!}(i_{r})_{!}(i_{r})^{-1}(K|_{U})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}\tilde{f}_{!}(K|_{U})

(1.33)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover for such
\tilde{f}
we have

{\rm SS}({\rm R}\tilde{f}_{!}(K|_{U}))\subset\varpi\rho^{-1}{\rm SS}(K|_{U}),

(1.34)

where we used the natural morphisms

T^{*}\mathbb{C}\overset{\varpi}{\longleftarrow}U\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\overset{\rho}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}U

(1.35)

associated to

\tilde{f}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

This in particular implies that for any
algebraic constructible sheaf

K\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
the geometric condition
of Kashiwara and Schapira
in
[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
is satisfied
by generic polynomial functions on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
To treat the singularities of
f^{w}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
w\in\Omega
)
at their points of indeterminacy,
we develop a general theory of meromorphic
vanishing cycle functors (with compact
support) by modifying the similar ones
studied previously by Raibaut
[
60
]

and Nguyen-Takeuchi
[
57
]
.
See Section
2
for the details.

It is clear that we can readily
extend Theorem
1.4
to all
holonomic D-modules on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
having
only exponentially twisted composition factors.
Recall that the Fourier
transform is an exact functor.
Moreover, if for a holonomic D-module

\mathcal{N}
on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
there exists an
enhanced sheaf
G
globally defined
on
X_{\mathbb{R}}
and having
a local expression similar to that of
the above one
F
such that

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{N})\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\rm E}_{X^{{\rm an}}}\overset{+}{\otimes}G,

(1.36)

then we can obtain a formula for
the enhanced ind-sheaf

\pi^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{V}\otimes\Big(Sol_{\overline{Y}}^{\rm E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}^{\wedge}})\Big)\quad\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}})

(1.37)

(
V
is an open subset of
Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
) on
\overline{Y}^{{\rm an}}

along the same line
as in the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
Recall that in
[
9
]
D’Agnolo and
Kashiwara proved that
for a complex manifold
X
and
an exponential D-module
\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{f}

on it associated to a meromorphic function

f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)
along
a closed hypersurface

D\subset X
and
U=X\setminus D

we have an isomorphism

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{f}\big)\simeq\mathbb{E}_{U|X}^{\rm Ref}\simeq\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \Big(\mathbb{C}_{\{(z,t)\in X\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ z\in U,t+{\rm Re}f(z)\geq a\}}\Big)

(1.38)

of enhanced ind-sheaves on
X^{{\rm an}}=X_{\mathbb{R}}
.
This formula for the enhanced solution complex

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{f}\big)
played
a central role in the proof of the main results
in
[
9
]
. In Section
7

we shall try to extend it to arbitrary meromorphic
connections. Our argument is a
higher-dimensional analogue of those
of Kashiwara-Schapira
[
30
, Section 7]

and Morando
[
50
, Section 2.1]
. We believe
that this approach would lead us to a
full generalization of Theorem
1.4
to arbitrary
holonomic D-modules on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

Acknowledgement:

The author thanks Professors Tat Thang Nguyen,
Adam Parusinski and Jörg Schürmann for
several discussions with them
during the preparation of this paper.

2
Meromorphic Nearby and Vanishing Cycle

Functors

In this section, we recall the definitions of the
meromorphic nearby cycle functors introduced in
Nguyen-Takeuchi
[
57
]
and
Raibaut
[
60
]
and prove their basic properties.
In this paper we essentially
follow the terminology of

[
12
]
,
[
21
]
and
[
27
]
.
For a topological space
X
denote by
{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
the
derived category whose objects are
bounded complexes of sheaves
of
\mathbb{C}_{X}
-modules on
X
. If
X
is a complex manifold,
we denote by
{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
the full
subcategory of
{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
consisting of
constructible objects and adopt the
convention that
\mathbb{C}_{X}[{\rm dim}X]\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

is a perverse sheaf on
X
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold and
P(z),Q(z)

holomorphic functions on it. Assume that
Q(z)

is not identically zero on each connected component
of
X
. Then we define a meromorphic function
f(z)
on

X
by

f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}\qquad(z\in X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)).

(2.1)

Let us set
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
.
If
P
and
Q
are coprime in the local ring

\mathcal{O}_{X,z}
at a point
z\in X
, then

I(f)
is nothing but the set of the indeterminacy
points of
f
on a neighborhood of
z
. Note that
the set
I(f)
depends on the pair
(P(z),Q(z))
of
holomorphic functions representing
f(z)
.
For example, if we take a holomorphic function

R(z)
on
X
(which is not identically zero on
each connected component of
X
) and set

g(z)=\frac{P(z)R(z)}{Q(z)R(z)}\qquad(z\in X\setminus(Q^{-1}(0)\cup R^{-1}(0))),

(2.2)

then the set
I(g)=I(f)\cup R^{-1}(0)
might be
bigger than
I(f)
. In this way, we
distinguish
f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}
from

g(z)=\frac{P(z)R(z)}{Q(z)R(z)}
even if
their values coincide over an open dense
subset of
X
.
This is the convention due to
Gusein-Zade, Luengo and Melle-Hernández

[
16
]
etc.
Now we recall the following
fundamental theorem due to
[
16
]

and
[
17
]
. In what follows, we assume that

X
is connected and
P
is not identically zero on it.

Theorem 2.1
.

(Gusein-Zade, Luengo and Melle-Hernández

[
16
]
,
[
17
]
)
For any point
z_{0}\in P^{-1}(0)
there exists

\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}

and the open ball
B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})\subset X
of
radius
\varepsilon>0
with center at
z_{0}

(in a local chart of
X
) the restriction

B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

(2.3)

of
f:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a locally trivial
fibration over a sufficiently small
punctured disk in
\mathbb{C}
with center at
the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}

We call the fiber in this theorem the Milnor fiber of
the meromorphic function
f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}

at
z_{0}\in P^{-1}(0)
and denote it by
F_{z_{0}}
.
For the meromorphic function
f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}
let

i_{f}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}

(2.4)

be the (not necessarily)
closed embedding defined by
z\longmapsto(z,f(z))
.
Let
\tau:X\times\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the
second projection. Then
for
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
we set

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero}}(\mathcal{F}):=\psi_{\tau}({\rm R}i_{f*}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}))\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

(2.5)

(see Nguyen-Takeuchi
[
57
]
).
We call
\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero}}(\mathcal{F})
the meromorphic
nearby cycle sheaf of
\mathcal{F}
along
f
. Moreover we set

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F}):=\psi_{\tau}(i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}))\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

(2.6)

(see Raibaut
[
60
]
) and call it the meromorphic
nearby cycle sheaf with compact support
of
\mathcal{F}
along
f
. Then the proof of
the following lemma is similar to those
of
[
57
, Lemma 2.1 and Remark 2.3]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

(i)

The support of
\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})
is
contained in
P^{-1}(0)
.

(ii)

There exists an isomorphism

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F}_{X\setminus(P^{-1}(0)\cup Q^{-1}(0))}).

(2.7)

(iii)

Assume that the meromorphic
function
f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}

is holomorphic on a neighborhood of a point

z_{0}\in X
i.e. there exists a holomorphic function

g(z)
defined on a neighborhood of

z_{0}\in X
such that
P(z)=Q(z)\cdot g(z)
on it.
Then
we have an isomorphism

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq\psi_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})_{z_{0}}

(2.8)

for the classical (holomorphic) nearby cycle
functor
\psi_{g}(\cdot)
.

(iv)

For any point
z_{0}\in P^{-1}(0)
and

j\in\mathbb{Z}
we have an isomorphism

H^{j}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq H^{j}_{c}(\overline{F_{z_{0}}}\setminus Q^{-1}(0);\mathcal{F}),

(2.9)

where
H^{j}_{c}(\cdot)
stands for the hypercohomology
group with compact support.

Example 2.3
.

Consider the case where
X
is the complex plane
\mathbb{C}^{2}

endowed with the standard coordinate
z=(z_{1},z_{2})=(x,y)
and
let
P,Q\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
be polynomials on
X=\mathbb{C}^{2}

coprime each other such that
P(0)=Q(0)=0
. Assume that
the complex curve
P^{-1}(0)\subset X
(resp.
Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
)
has an isolated singular point (resp. is smooth)
at the origin
0=(0,0)\in X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Then for
c\in\mathbb{C}

the fiber
f^{-1}(c)\subset X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
of the
rational function
f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is explicitly described as follows:

f^{-1}(c)=\{(x,y)\in X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\ |\ P(x,y)-c\cdot Q(x,y)=0\}\quad\subset X\setminus Q^{-1}(0).

(2.10)

On the other hand, by
[
46
, Corollary 2.8]
the set

\Sigma_{f}\subset\mathbb{C}
of the critical values of

f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is finite and hence generic
fibers of
f
are smooth. This implies that for

c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that

0<|c|\ll 1
the complex curve

\overline{f^{-1}(c)}=\{(x,y)\in X=\mathbb{C}^{2}\ |\ P(x,y)-c\cdot Q(x,y)=0\}\quad\subset X

(2.11)

is smooth outside the origin
0=(0,0)\in X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Moreover by the conditions

\Bigl(\frac{\partial P}{\partial x}(0,0),\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}(0,0)\Bigr)=(0,0),\quad\Bigl(\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x}(0,0),\frac{\partial Q}{\partial y}(0,0)\Bigr)\not=(0,0)

(2.12)

we see that for such
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
the complex curve

\overline{f^{-1}(c)}\subset X
is smooth also at
the origin. For a complex number
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and

\varepsilon>0
such that
0<|c|\ll\varepsilon\ll 1

let us explain the topology of the Milnor fiber

F_{0}=(B_{\varepsilon}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\cap f^{-1}(c)=(B_{\varepsilon}(0)\cap\overline{f^{-1}(c)})\setminus\{0\}

(2.13)

of
f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
at the origin
0=(0,0)\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)
. We set
L:=P^{-1}(0)\cap\partial B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subset S_{\varepsilon}(0):=\partial B_{\varepsilon}(0)\simeq S^{3}

and call it the link of the complex curve

P^{-1}(0)\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
at the origin. The small
sphere
S_{\varepsilon}(0)
being compact,
the boundary
\partial\overline{F_{0}}
of the closure

\overline{F_{0}}
of
F_{0}
is homeomorphic to
L
.
We know also that the link
L
is
a disjoint union of some circles
S^{1}
embedded in

S_{\varepsilon}(0)\simeq S^{3}
. We denote the number of
circles
S^{1}
in
L
by
m
and shall explain how to
calculate it from now. For this purpose, let

F(P)_{0}
be the Milnor fiber of
P^{-1}(0)\subset X

at the origin
0=(0,0)\in X
and consider the
monodromy automorphism

\Phi(P)_{1}:H^{1}(F(P)_{0};\mathbb{C})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}H^{1}(F(P)_{0};\mathbb{C})

(2.14)

of its first cohomology group
H^{1}(F(P)_{0};\mathbb{C})
.
Denote by
N_{1}
the number of the Jordan blocks for
the eigenvalue
1
in
\Phi(P)_{1}
. Then by the results in

[
46
, Section 8]
(see also the proof of

[
51
, Lemma 4.3]
) we have
m=N_{1}+1
. Since by
the classical theory of the mixed Hodge structure
of
H^{1}(F(P)_{0};\mathbb{C})
we know in this case of

{\rm dim}X=2
that the maximal possible size of such
Jordan blocks is one, we can calculate the number

N_{1}
by the theory of monodromy zeta functions
(see
[
66
, Section 2]
for a review of this
subject). We thus have seen that the interior of

\overline{F_{0}}
is a smooth Riemann surface with
the boundary
\partial\overline{F_{0}}
which is
isomorphic to the disjoint union

S^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup S^{1}
of
N_{1}+1
copies of

S^{1}
. Let
M
be a compact oriented surface obtained by
attaching
N_{1}+1
(
2
-dimensional) disks to

\overline{F_{0}}
along its boundary

\partial\overline{F_{0}}
and denote its genus
by
g(M)
. By taking a triangulation of
\overline{F_{0}}

we see that

H_{c}^{2}(\overline{F_{0}};\mathbb{C}_{X})\simeq H^{2}(\overline{F_{0}};\mathbb{C}_{X})\simeq 0
. Then by
the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{M\setminus\overline{F_{0}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{\overline{F_{0}}}\longrightarrow 0

(2.15)

we obtain isomorphisms

H^{j}(\overline{F_{0}};\mathbb{C}_{X})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}&(j=0)\\
\\
\mathbb{C}^{2g(M)+N_{1}}&(j=1)\\
\\
0&(\mbox{otherwise}).\end{cases}

(2.16)

Moreover, by
\overline{F_{0}}\setminus Q^{-1}(0)=\overline{F_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
we can show that
there exist isomorphisms

H^{j}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}\simeq H_{c}^{j}(\overline{F_{0}}\setminus Q^{-1}(0);\mathbb{C}_{X})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}^{2g(M)+N_{1}}&(j=1)\\
\\
0&(\mbox{otherwise}).\end{cases}

(2.17)

Compare this result with the perversity of

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X}[1])
which will be proved
soon below in this section. For the above
c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and

\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
such that

0<\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll|c|\ll\varepsilon
we set

G_{0}:=(B_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\cap f^{-1}(c)=(B_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(0)\cap\overline{f^{-1}(c)})\setminus\{0\}.

(2.18)

Then the closure
\overline{G_{0}}
of
G_{0}

is homeomorphic to the (
2
-dimensional) disk and

\overline{G_{0}}\setminus Q^{-1}(0)=\overline{G_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. We thus obtain
isomorphisms

H_{c}^{j}(\overline{G_{0}}\setminus Q^{-1}(0);\mathbb{C}_{X})\simeq 0\qquad(j\in\mathbb{Z}).

(2.19)

The difference of the topology of
G_{0}
from that of
F_{0}

comes from the fact that there exists
r>0
such that

\varepsilon^{\prime}<r<\varepsilon
and
the sphere
S_{r}(0)=\partial B_{r}(0)
is tangent to
the smooth curve
\overline{f^{-1}(c)}
.
This explains the reason why one should distinguish
F_{0}

from
G_{0}
carefully in the study of the Milnor fiber
F_{0}
of

f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

The following result is an analogue
of
[
57
, Theorem 2.2]
for the meromorphic
nearby cycle sheaf with compact support
of
\mathcal{F}
along
f
.

Theorem 2.4
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is constructible, then

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is also constructible.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is perverse, then

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})[-1]\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is also perverse.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is
constructible. Define a hypersurface
W
of

X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}
by

W=\{(z,\tau)\in X\times\mathbb{C}\ |\ P(z)-\tau Q(z)=0\}

(2.20)

and let
\kappa:W\rightarrow X
be the
restriction of the first projection

X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}\rightarrow X
to it.
Then
\kappa
induces an isomorphism

\kappa^{-1}(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)

(2.21)

and
\kappa^{-1}(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))

is nothing but the graph

\{(z,f(z))\in(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\times\mathbb{C}\ |\ z\in X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\}

(2.22)

of
f:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
In this way, we identify
X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)

and the open subset
\kappa^{-1}(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))

of
W
. Let

j_{f}:(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}

(2.23)

and
i_{W}:W\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}

be the inclusion maps. Then as in the proof
of
[
57
, Theorem 2.2]

we obtain an isomorphism

\mathcal{G}:=i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\simeq j_{f_{!}}j_{f}^{-1}(i_{W*}\kappa^{-1}\mathcal{F}).

(2.24)

From this we see that
\mathcal{G}=i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})
is constructible.
Now the assertion (i) is clear.
Assume that
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
is perverse.
Then, although
\mathcal{H}:=i_{W*}\kappa^{-1}\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})
is not
necessarily perverse (up to some shift)
in general, its
restriction

j_{f}^{-1}\mathcal{H}\simeq\mathcal{H}|_{(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}}

(2.25)

to
(X\setminus Q^{-1}(0))\times\mathbb{C}_{t}
is preverse
(up to some shift). Moreover, its Verdier dual

\mathbb{D}_{X\times\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H})\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})

satisfies the same property. It follows
from the proof of
[
57
, Theorem 2.2 (ii)]
that

{\rm R}j_{f_{*}}j_{f}^{-1}\mathbb{D}_{X\times\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H})\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})

(2.26)

is perverse (up to some shift). By the isomorphism

\mathbb{D}_{X\times\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{D}_{X\times\mathbb{C}}(j_{f_{!}}j_{f}^{-1}(\mathcal{H}))\simeq{\rm R}j_{f_{*}}j_{f}^{-1}\mathbb{D}_{X\times\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}),

(2.27)

we find that
\mathcal{G}=j_{f_{!}}j_{f}^{-1}(\mathcal{H})\simeq i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})

is also perverse (up to some shift).
Then the assertion of (ii) immediately follows from
the t-exactness of the nearby cycle functor

\psi_{\tau}(\cdot):{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X).

(2.28)

∎

By this theorem we obtain a functor

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot):{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\longrightarrow{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X).

(2.29)

Let
i_{0}:X\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}_{\tau}

(
x\longmapsto(x,0)
) be the inclusion map
and for
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
set

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F}):=\phi_{\tau}(i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}))\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X).

(2.30)

We call it the meromorphic
vanishing cycle sheaf with compact support
of
\mathcal{F}
along
f
. Then there exists a
distinguished triangle

i_{0}^{-1}i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\longrightarrow\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\longrightarrow\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\overset{+1}{\longrightarrow}

(2.31)

in
{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
. Moreover, since for the inclusion map

j:P^{-1}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\hookrightarrow X
we have a Cartesian diagram

\begin{CD}P^{-1}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0)@>{j}>{}>X\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{i_{0}}V\\
X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)@>{i_{f}}>{}>X\times\mathbb{C}_{t}\end{CD}

(2.32)

of inclusion maps, we obtain an isomorphism

i_{0}^{-1}i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\simeq j_{!}(\mathcal{F}|_{P^{-1}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\simeq\mathcal{F}_{P^{-1}(0)\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}.

(2.33)

We thus see that also the functor

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot):{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)\longrightarrow{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

(2.34)

preserves the constructibility and
the perversity (up to some shift).
Moreover by (
2.31
) and (
2.33
),
for any
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
we have isomorphisms

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\qquad(z_{0}\in Q^{-1}(0)).

(2.35)

The proof of the following lemma is similar to those
of
[
57
, Lemma 2.1 and Remark 2.3]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(i)

The support of
\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})
is
contained in
P^{-1}(0)
.

(ii)

There exists an isomorphism

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F}_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}).

(2.36)

(iii)

Assume that the meromorphic
function
f(z)=\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}

is holomorphic on a neighborhood of a point

z_{0}\in X
i.e. there exists a holomorphic function

g(z)
defined on a neighborhood of

z_{0}\in X
such that
P(z)=Q(z)\cdot g(z)
on it.
Then
we have an isomorphism

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq\phi_{g}(\mathcal{F}_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})_{z_{0}}

(2.37)

for the classical (holomorphic) vanishing cycle
functor
\phi_{g}(\cdot)
.

The following result is a cohomological generalization of
Theorem
2.1
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
be a constructible sheaf on
X
.
Then for any
z_{0}\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)

and
c\in\mathbb{C}
there exist small
0<\varepsilon\ll 1

such that the cohomology sheaves
H^{j}{\rm R}f_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)

(j\in\mathbb{Z})
are local systems over a sufficiently small punctured
disk centered at
c\in\mathbb{C}
. Moreover we have

{\rm R}f_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)_{c}\simeq{\rm R}(f-c)_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)_{0}\simeq 0

(2.38)

and there exist isomorphisms

\displaystyle\phi_{\tau-c}\Bigl({\rm R}f_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)\Bigr)\simeq\psi_{\tau-c}\Bigl({\rm R}f_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)\Bigr)

(2.39)

\displaystyle\simeq\psi_{f-c}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq\phi_{f-c}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}.

(2.40)

Proof.

Note that for
P_{c}:=P-c\cdot Q
we have

f-c=\frac{P_{c}}{Q}
. Then we obtain the first
assertion by (the proof of) Lemma
2.2
(iv)
(see also the proof of
[
66
, Theorem 2.6]
).
We can also show the vanishing (
2.38
)
by the cone theorem proved by
[
46
, Theorem 2.10]
and

[
7
, Lemma 3.2]
. Indeed, let

X=\sqcup_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
be a
stratification of
X
such that
H^{j}(\mathcal{F}|_{X_{\alpha}})

is locally constant for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\alpha\in A
.
Then the cone theorem implies that there exist small
0<\varepsilon\ll 1

such that for any
\alpha\in A
the subsets

\overline{X_{\alpha}}\cap P_{c}^{-1}(0)\cap\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})},\qquad\overline{X_{\alpha}}\cap P_{c}^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)\cap\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}

(2.41)

of
\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}
are contractible and hence

{\rm R}(f-c)_{!}\bigl(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{X_{\alpha}}\cap\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})}}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)}\bigr)_{0}\simeq 0.

(2.42)

By decomposing the support of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to the
stratification
X=\sqcup_{\alpha\in A}X_{\alpha}
of
X

we thus can prove the vanishing (
2.38
).
Together with Lemma
2.2
(iv), we obtain also
the remaining assertions.
∎

Proposition 2.7
.

For a constructible sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
on
X
and a
point
z_{0}\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)
,
there exists a finite subset
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{C}

of
\mathbb{C}
such that we have

\phi_{f-c}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq 0.

(2.43)

for any
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
.

Proof.

Note that for the point
z_{0}\in I(f)

(under the natural identification of
X\times\{0\}
with

X\times\{c\}
) we have an
isomorphism

\phi_{f-c}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq\phi_{\tau-c}\Bigl(i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\Bigr)_{(z_{0},c)}.

(2.44)

Let
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}=\mathbb{C}\sqcup\{\infty\}
be the
projective compactification of
\mathbb{C}
and

i:X\times\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow X\times{\mathbb{P}}
the
inclusion map. Then by the proof of Theorem
2.4
(i)
we see also that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}:=i_{!}\Bigl(i_{f!}(\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)})\Bigr)\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X\times{\mathbb{P}})
is constructible. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a Whitney stratification
of
X\times{\mathbb{P}}
adapted to
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
such that

{\rm SS}(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}})\subset\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}T^{*}_{S}(X\times{\mathbb{P}}).

(2.45)

Then by the theorem in
[
15
, page 43]
, after
refining
\mathcal{S}
if necessary, we may assume that
for
\mathcal{S}
and a Whitney stratification
\mathcal{S}_{0}
of

X
the projection
X\times{\mathbb{P}}\longrightarrow X

is a stratified fiber bundle as in its assertion.
We may assume also that the one point set

\{z_{0}\}\subset X
is a stratum of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.
Then there exists a finite subset

\{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{k}\}\subset{\mathbb{P}}
of
{\mathbb{P}}

such that the strata in
\mathcal{S}
projecting to
the one
\{z_{0}\}\subset X
in
\mathcal{S}_{0}
are

\{(z_{0},c_{i})\}\subset X\times{\mathbb{P}}

(
1\leq i\leq k
) and
\{z_{0}\}\times({\mathbb{P}}\setminus\{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{k}\})\subset X\times{\mathbb{P}}
.
Let us set
\Sigma:=\mathbb{C}\cap\{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{k}\}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Then by
(
2.44
), (
2.45
) and
[
27
, Proposition 8.6.3]

we obtain the desired vanishing

\phi_{f-c}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq 0.

(2.46)

for any
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
.
∎

The following result is an analogue
for
\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot)
and

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot)
of the
classical one for
\psi_{f}(\cdot)

and
\phi_{f}(\cdot)

(see e.g.
[
12
, Proposition 4.2.11]
,

[
27
, Exercise VIII.15]

and
[
57
, Proposition 2.4]
).

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\nu:Y\longrightarrow X
be a proper surjective
morphism of complex manifolds and

f\circ\nu
the meromorphic function on
Y
defined by

f\circ\nu=\frac{P\circ\nu}{Q\circ\nu}.

(2.47)

Then we have:

(i)

For
\mathcal{G}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(Y)

there exists isomorphisms

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}({\rm R}\nu_{*}\mathcal{G})\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}\psi_{f\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{G}),\qquad\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}({\rm R}\nu_{*}\mathcal{G})\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}\phi_{f\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{G}).

(2.48)

(ii)

If moreover
\nu
induces an isomorphism

Y\setminus\nu^{-1}(P^{-1}(0)\cup Q^{-1}(0))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X\setminus(P^{-1}(0)\cup Q^{-1}(0)),

(2.49)

then for
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

there exists an isomorphism

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}\psi_{f\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{F}).

(2.50)

(iii)

If moreover
\nu
induces an isomorphism

Y\setminus\nu^{-1}Q^{-1}(0)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}X\setminus Q^{-1}(0),

(2.51)

then for
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

there exists an isomorphism

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}\phi_{f\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\nu^{-1}\mathcal{F}).

(2.52)

Lemma 2.9
.

Assume that the hypersurfaces
P^{-1}(0),Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
are smooth and
intersect transversally in a neighborhood
U\subset X
of a point
z_{0}\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)
.
Then we have

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{z_{0}}\simeq\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{z_{0}}\simeq 0.

(2.53)

Proof.

Let
\nu:Y\longrightarrow U
be the blow-up of

U
along the complex submanifold

I(f)\cap U\subset U
of codimension two.
Then
\nu
induces an isomorphism

Y\setminus\nu^{-1}(Q|_{U})^{-1}(0)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}U\setminus(Q|_{U})^{-1}(0)

(2.54)

and we can apply Proposition
2.8
(iii)
to the meromorphic function

f|_{U}:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and

\nu:Y\longrightarrow U
to obtain an isomorphism

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{z_{0}}=\phi_{f|_{U}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{U})_{z_{0}}\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu^{-1}(z_{0});\phi_{(f|_{U})\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{Y})).

(2.55)

By our construction of the functor
\phi_{(f|_{U})\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot)
, the support of

\phi_{(f|_{U})\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{Y})
is
contained in the proper transform of

P^{-1}(0)\cap U
in
Y
.
Moreover by Lemma
2.5
(iii),
we can easily show that the stalk of

\phi_{(f|_{U})\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{Y})

at each point of
\nu^{-1}(z_{0})
is
isomorphic to zero
(see also e.g.
[
66
, Lemma 2.9]
).
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

For a point
z_{0}\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)

assume that there exists its neighborhood
U\subset X
such that the hypersurfaces
P^{-1}(0),Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
are smooth and
intersect transversally in
U\setminus\{z_{0}\}
.
Then we have the concentration

H^{j}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{z_{0}}\simeq H^{j}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{z_{0}}\simeq 0\qquad(j\not={\rm dim}X-1).

(2.56)

Proof.

By our assumption and Lemma
2.9
, the support of

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})|_{U}\simeq\phi_{f|_{U}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{U})

(2.57)

is contained in the one point set
\{z_{0}\}\subset U
. Then the assertion
immediately follows from the
perversity of

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X}[{\rm dim}X])[-1]
.
∎

By the proof of Corollary
2.10
, we obtain also the
following result.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
be a perverse sheaf on
X
and
for a point
z_{0}\in I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)

assume that there exists its neighborhood
U\subset X
such that the support of

\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K})|_{U}\simeq\phi_{f|_{U}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K}|_{U})

(2.58)

is contained in the one point set
\{z_{0}\}\subset U
.
Then we have the concentration

H^{j}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K})_{z_{0}}\simeq H^{j}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K})_{z_{0}}\simeq 0\qquad(j\not=-1).

(2.59)

In the situation of Proposition
2.11
,
we can calculate the dimension of the only
non-trivial cohomology group

H^{-1}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K})_{z_{0}}\simeq H^{-1}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{K})_{z_{0}}

(2.60)

as follows.
For a constructible sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
we set

\chi(\mathcal{F},z_{0}):=\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{j}{\rm dim}H^{j}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\qquad\in\mathbb{Z}

(2.61)

so that for any distinguished triangle

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\overset{+1}{\longrightarrow}
in
{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

we have
\chi(\mathcal{F},z_{0})=\chi(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},z_{0})+\chi(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime},z_{0})
.
Let us set
D:=P^{-1}(0)\cup Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
.
Then by Lemma
2.2
(ii) we may assume that

\mathcal{K}_{X\setminus D}\simeq\mathcal{K}
. By
decomposing the the support of
the perverse sheaf
\mathcal{K}_{X\setminus D}\simeq\mathcal{K}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)

with respect to a stratification
of
X
adapted to
\mathcal{K}
and the divisor
D\subset X

in
X
, it suffices to
calculate
\chi(\mathcal{F},z_{0})\in\mathbb{Z}
for some

\mathcal{F}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X)
such that for a
local system
L
on a stratum
S\subset X

contained in

X\setminus D
and the inclusion
map
j:S\hookrightarrow X
we have

\mathcal{F}=j_{!}L
. On the other hand, as in
the proof of
[
45
, Theorem 3.6]
, we can
construct a proper morphism

\nu_{1}:\widetilde{X}_{1}\longrightarrow X

of a complex manifold
\widetilde{X}_{1}

which induces an isomorphism over the open
subset
X\setminus D\subset X

such that the divisor
D^{\prime}:=\nu_{1}^{-1}(D)=(P\circ\nu_{1})^{-1}(0)\cup(Q\circ\nu_{1})^{-1}(0)
in
\widetilde{X}_{1}
is
normal crossing and the rational function

f\circ\nu_{1}:=(P\circ\nu_{1})/(Q\circ\nu_{1})

on
\widetilde{X}_{1}
has no point of indeterminacy
on the whole
\widetilde{X}_{1}
. Namely
the pole and zero divisors of
the rational function

f\circ\nu_{1}
are disjoint in
D^{\prime}
.
Let
g:\widetilde{X}_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}

be the holomorphic map defined by
f\circ\nu_{1}
.
In this situation, there exists a (not necessarily closed)
embedding
i_{S}:S\hookrightarrow\widetilde{X}_{1}

such that
\nu_{1}\circ i_{S}=j
. Then we have

\nu_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{F}=\nu_{1}^{-1}j_{!}L\simeq i_{S!}L
and hence by Proposition
2.8
(ii)
and Lemma
2.2
(iii) we obtain an isomorphism

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu_{1}^{-1}(z_{0})\cap g^{-1}(0);\psi_{g}(i_{S!}L)).

(2.62)

We set
S_{1}:=i_{S}(S)\subset\widetilde{X}_{1}
.
Denote by
\overline{S_{1}}
its closure in
\widetilde{X}_{1}

and let
i_{\overline{S_{1}}}:\overline{S_{1}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{X}_{1}

be the inclusion map. Then there exists a proper morphism

\nu_{2}:T\longrightarrow\overline{S_{1}}

of a complex manifold
T

which induces an isomorphism over the open
subset
S_{1}
of
\overline{S_{1}}

such that
E:=\nu_{2}^{-1}(\overline{S_{1}}\setminus S_{1})\subset T
is a normal crossing divisor in
T
.
Set
T^{\circ}:=T\setminus E\simeq S_{1}\simeq S
and let
\iota:T^{\circ}\hookrightarrow T

be the inclusion map.
Then for the proper morphism
\nu:=\nu_{1}\circ i_{\overline{S_{1}}}\circ\nu_{2}:T\longrightarrow X

and
\widetilde{g}:=g\circ i_{\overline{S_{1}}}\circ\nu_{2}=(g|_{\overline{S_{1}}})\circ\nu_{2}:T\longrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}
we
obtain an isomorphism

\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathcal{F})_{z_{0}}\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu^{-1}(z_{0})\cap\widetilde{g}^{-1}(0);\psi_{\widetilde{g}}(\iota_{!}L)).

(2.63)

Since the divisor
E\subset T
is
normal crossing, one can calculate

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{j}{\rm dim}H^{j}(\nu^{-1}(z_{0})\cap\widetilde{g}^{-1}(0);\psi_{\widetilde{g}}(\iota_{!}L))\quad\in\mathbb{Z}

(2.64)

by the results in
[
43
, Section 5]

(see also e.g.
[
66
, Lemma 2.18]
).

Example 2.12
.

As in Example
2.3
consider the case where

X
is the complex plane
\mathbb{C}^{2}

endowed with the standard coordinate
z=(z_{1},z_{2})=(x,y)
and
for two non-zero complex numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{C}^{*}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
set
P(x,y)=ax^{2}+bxy+y^{3}
,
Q(x,y)=x
and

f(x,y)=\frac{P(x,y)}{Q(x,y)}=\frac{ax^{2}+bxy+y^{3}}{x}\qquad((x,y)\in X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)).

(2.65)

Then the complex curve
P^{-1}(0)\subset X
(resp.
Q^{-1}(0)\subset X
)
has an isolated singular point (resp. is smooth)
at the origin
0=(0,0)\in X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Namely the rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfies the conditions in Example
2.3
.
Since moreover in this case
P^{-1}(0)\subset X
is
Newton non-degenerate at the origin (see
[
66
, Definition 3.5]

for the definition), by
[
66
, Theorem 8.9 (i)]

the number
N_{1}
of the Jordan blocks for
the eigenvalue
1
in its first Milnor monodromy is
equal to
1
. As we explained in Example
2.3
,
we can show it also by Varchenko’s formula for
monodromy zeta functions in
[
69
]
.
Hence in this case, by the arguments in
Example
2.3
the boundary
\partial\overline{F_{0}}

of the closure
\overline{F_{0}}
of
the Milnor fiber
F_{0}
of
f
at the origin
0=(0,0)\in I(f)

is homeomorphic to the disjoint union

S^{1}\sqcup S^{1}
of two copies of
S^{1}
and
there exist isomorphisms

H^{j}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}\simeq H^{j}\phi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}^{2g(M)+1}&(j=1)\\
\\
0&(\mbox{otherwise}),\end{cases}

(2.66)

where
g(M)
is the genus of the
compact oriented surface
M
obtained by
attaching two (
2
-dimensional) disks to

\overline{F_{0}}
along its boundary

\partial\overline{F_{0}}
. From now, we shall
show that
g(M)
is equal to
0
. For this purpose, we set

D_{1}:=P^{-1}(0)
,
D_{2}:=Q^{-1}(0)
and

D:=D_{1}\cup D_{2}
and construct
a proper morphism
\nu:Y\longrightarrow X

of a
2
-dimensional complex manifold
Y

which induces an isomorphism over the open
subset
X\setminus D\subset X

such that the divisor
\nu^{-1}(D)
in
Y
is
normal crossing as follows. First, let
\nu_{1}:X_{1}\longrightarrow X

be the blow-up of
X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
along the origin

\{0\}\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
E_{1}:=\nu_{1}^{-1}(0)(\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\subset X_{1}
its exceptional divisor.
We denote the proper transforms of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}

in
X_{1}
by
\widetilde{D_{1}}\subset X_{1}
and

\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{1}
respectively.
Then we can easily see that the set

E_{1}\cap\widetilde{D_{1}}
consists of
two points and one of them is the unique point
in the one point set
E_{1}\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}

and the other one is in
E_{1}\setminus\widetilde{D_{2}}
.
Let
\nu_{2}:X_{2}\longrightarrow X_{1}

be the blow-up of
X_{1}
along the point

E_{1}\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{1}
and

E_{2}(\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\subset X_{2}
its exceptional divisor.
By abuse of notations, we denote the proper transform
of
\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{1}

in
X_{2}
also by
\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{2}
.
Then the set
E_{2}\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}
consists of
one point and the meromorphic function

f\circ\nu_{1}\circ\nu_{2}
on
X_{2}
has a zero
(resp. pole) of order
1
along the divisor

E_{2}\subset X_{2}
(resp.
\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{2}
).
This implies that
f\circ\nu_{1}\circ\nu_{2}
still has
indeterminacy at the point
E_{2}\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}
.
In order to eliminate it, let us consider the
blow-up
\nu_{3}:X_{3}\longrightarrow X_{2}

of
X_{2}
along the point
E_{2}\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset X_{2}
. Set
Y:=X_{3}
and
\nu:=\nu_{1}\circ\nu_{2}\circ\nu_{3}:Y\longrightarrow X
and
let
E_{3}(\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1})\subset Y
be the exceptional divisor of
the last bolw-up
\nu_{3}:Y=X_{3}\longrightarrow X_{2}
.
Again by abuse of notations, we denote the proper
transforms of
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
,
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
in
Y=X_{3}
by

\widetilde{D_{1}}
,
\widetilde{D_{2}}
,
\widetilde{E_{1}}
and

\widetilde{E_{2}}
respectively.
Then the meromorphic function
f\circ\nu
on
Y

has a zero (resp. pole) of order
1
along the divisors

\widetilde{D_{1}},\widetilde{E_{1}},\widetilde{E_{2}}\subset Y
(resp. along the divisor
\widetilde{D_{2}}\subset Y
). Moreover
f\circ\nu
has neither zero
nor pole along the divisor
E_{3}\subset Y
and we have

(\widetilde{D_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}})\cap\widetilde{D_{2}}=\emptyset
. This implies that
the meromorphic function
f\circ\nu
has no point
of indeterminacy on the whole
Y
. Moreover
the divisor

D^{\prime}:=\nu^{-1}(D)=(\widetilde{D_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}})\cup E_{3}\cup\widetilde{D_{2}}\qquad\subset Y

(2.67)

in
Y
is normal crossing. Note that among the smooth divisors

\widetilde{D_{1}},\widetilde{E_{1}}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1},\widetilde{E_{2}}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1},E_{3}\simeq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in
Y
we have only the following four
intersection points:

A:=\widetilde{D_{1}}\cap\widetilde{E_{1}},\ B:=\widetilde{D_{1}}\cap\widetilde{E_{2}},\ A^{\prime}:=\widetilde{E_{1}}\cap\widetilde{E_{2}},\ B^{\prime}:=\widetilde{E_{2}}\cap E_{3}

(2.68)

and we set

\widetilde{E_{1}^{\circ}}:=\widetilde{E_{1}}\setminus\{A,A^{\prime}\},\qquad\widetilde{E_{2}^{\circ}}:=\widetilde{E_{2}}\setminus\{B,A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\}.

(2.69)

Let
g:Y\longrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
be the holomorphic map defined by
the meromorphic function
f\circ\nu
. Then we have

g^{-1}(0)=\widetilde{D_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}},\qquad\nu^{-1}(0)=\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}}\cup E_{3}

(2.70)

and hence by Lemma
2.2
(i), (ii), (iii)
and Proposition
2.8
(i) we obtain isomorphisms

\displaystyle\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}

\displaystyle\simeq\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X\setminus D})_{0}\simeq\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}({\rm R}\nu_{*}\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}})_{0}

(2.71)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu^{-1}(0);\psi_{f\circ\nu}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))

(2.72)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu^{-1}(0);\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))

(2.73)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\nu^{-1}(0)\cap g^{-1}(0);\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))

(2.74)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}};\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}})).

(2.75)

Since we know that the complex
\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}
is
concentrated in degree
1
, we obtain also

{\rm dim}H^{1}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}=-\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{j}{\rm dim}H^{j}(\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}};\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}})).

(2.76)

Now let
\chi(\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))

be the
\mathbb{Z}
-valued constructible function on

g^{-1}(0)
obtained by taking the local Euler-Poincaré
index of
\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}})
at
each point of
g^{-1}(0)
. Then we have an equality

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{j}{\rm dim}H^{j}(\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}};\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))=\int_{\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}}}\chi(\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}})),

(2.77)

where
\int_{\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}}}(\cdot)
stands for
the topological (Euler) integral over the
analytic subset
\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}}\subset g^{-1}(0)
.
Moreover by the results in
[
43
, Section 5]

we can easily show that

\chi(\psi_{g}(\mathbb{C}_{Y\setminus D^{\prime}}))|_{\widetilde{E_{1}}\cup\widetilde{E_{2}}}={\bf 1}_{\widetilde{E_{1}^{\circ}}}+{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{E_{2}^{\circ}}}.

(2.78)

Together with (
2.69
) we thus
obtain the desired equality

{\rm dim}H^{1}\psi_{f}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\mathbb{C}_{X})_{0}=-\chi(\widetilde{E_{1}^{\circ}})-\chi(\widetilde{E_{2}^{\circ}})=1.

(2.79)

3
Singularities at Infinity of
Meromorphic Functions

In this section, we study the singularities at
infinity of rational and
meromorphic functions on complex
affine subvarieties of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
.
Although we treat only rational functions here,
as is clear from the proofs our results
hold true also for (possibly multi-valued)
meromorphic functions with moderate growth
at infinity. We leave their precise formulations
to the readers. First, let us consider
a complex submanifold
S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
of

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
. Denote by
X_{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
S_{\mathbb{R}}
)
the underlying real analytic manifold of
X

(resp.
S
). Then for any point
z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}

the tangent space
T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
is naturally
identified with a linear subspace of

T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
. In fact,
we can easily see that it has also
a structure of
\mathbb{C}
-linear subspace of
dimension
{\rm dim}S
i.e.
T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}S}
.
For
u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}),v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
we set

\langle u,v\rangle:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}u_{i}v_{i}\qquad\in\mathbb{C}.

(3.1)

Let

(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\qquad((u,v)\longmapsto\langle u,\overline{v}\rangle)

(3.2)

be the standard Hermitian inner product of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
define its norm
||z||\geq 0
by

||z||=\sqrt{(z,z)}
. For a point
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N}

set
V:=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
and denote by

V^{*}
its dual space
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(V,\mathbb{R})\simeq T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then by the perfect pairing

{\rm Re}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
we obtain an isomorphism

\phi:V=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V^{*}=T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\qquad(v\longmapsto\{u\mapsto{\rm Re}(u,v)\}).

(3.3)

Define a real-valued function
\delta:X_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\delta(z):=\frac{1}{2}||z||^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(z,z).

(3.4)

Then for any point
z\in X_{\mathbb{R}}
, the
cotangent vector
d\delta(z)\in T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}

corresponds to the point
z\in T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}

itself via the isomorphism

\phi:V=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}V^{*}=T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Fix a point
z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}\subset X_{\mathbb{R}}
and
set
W:=T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}S}\subset V:=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Denote by
V^{*}

(resp.
W^{*}
) the dual space

{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(V,\mathbb{R})\simeq T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
{\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(W,\mathbb{R})\simeq T^{*}_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
). Then by the perfect pairing

{\rm Re}(\cdot,\cdot):W\times W\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
we obtain also an isomorphism

\psi:W=T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}W^{*}=T^{*}_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\Psi_{z}:V^{*}=T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow W^{*}=T^{*}_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
be the (
\mathbb{R}
-linear) morphism
induced by the inclusion map
S_{\mathbb{R}}\hookrightarrow X_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then there exists a
\mathbb{C}
-linear morphism

\Phi_{z}:V=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow W=T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
such that the diagram

\begin{CD}V@>{\phi}>{\sim}>V^{*}\\
@V{\Phi_{z}}V{}V@V{}V{\Psi_{z}}V\\
W@>{\psi}>{\sim}>W^{*}\end{CD}

(3.5)

communtes.

Proof.

Assume first that
S
is a complex hypersurface in

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. For a holomorphic function

h
defined on a neighborhood of the point

z\in S
in
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that
S=h^{-1}(0)

we set

{\rm grad}h(z):=\Bigl(\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{1}},\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{2}},\ldots\ldots,\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{N}}\Bigr)\in\mathbb{C}^{N}.

(3.6)

Then for any smooth curve
p(t):(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow S_{\mathbb{R}}

(
\varepsilon>0
)
such that
p(0)=z
we have

\frac{d}{dt}h(p(t))|_{t=0}=\Bigl\langle\frac{dp}{dt}(0),{\rm grad}h(z)\Bigr\rangle=0.

(3.7)

This implies that the
\mathbb{C}
-linear subspace

W=T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\subset V=T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}

is explicitly given by

W=\{v\in V\ |\ \langle v,{\rm grad}h(z)\rangle=(v,\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)})=0\},

(3.8)

where we set

\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)}:=\Bigl(\overline{\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{1}}},\overline{\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{2}}},\ldots\ldots,\overline{\frac{\partial h}{\partial z_{N}}}\Bigr)\in\mathbb{C}^{N}.

(3.9)

Hence we can define a
\mathbb{C}
-linear morphism

\Phi_{z}:V\longrightarrow W
by
the formula

\Phi_{z}(v)=v-\frac{(v,\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)})}{(\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)},\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)})}\cdot\overline{{\rm grad}h(z)}\quad\in W.

(3.10)

Then it is straightforward to check that
the diagram (
3.5
) commutes.
If
{\rm dim}S<{\rm dim}X-1=N-1
, we repeat this
argument.
∎

In what follows, we assume that

S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a smooth Zariski
locally closed (complex) affine subvariety
i.e. a smooth quasi-affine subvariety of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
p(t):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
be an analytic curve on
S

such that

\lim_{t\to+0}||p(t)||=+\infty

(3.11)

and its expansion at infinity is of the form

p(t)=at^{\alpha}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(a\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\},\alpha<0).

(3.12)

Define an analytic curve

q(t):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
by

q(t)=\Phi_{p(t)}(p(t))\qquad(0<t<\varepsilon).

(3.13)

Recall that the element
d\delta(p(t))\in T^{*}_{p(t)}X_{\mathbb{R}}

corresponds to
p(t)\in T_{p(t)}X_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}

via the isomorphism

T_{p(t)}X_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}_{p(t)}X_{\mathbb{R}}

and the point
\Phi_{p(t)}(p(t))\in T_{p(t)}S_{\mathbb{R}}

is considered as an element of
T_{p(t)}X_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
Then the expansion at infinity of
q(t)

has the same top term as that of
p(t)
:

q(t)=at^{\alpha}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(a\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\},\alpha<0).

(3.14)

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.1
,
assume first that
S
is a complex hypersurface in

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Consider a holomorphic function

h
defined on a neighborhood of a point

z\in S
in
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that
S=h^{-1}(0)
.
First of all, note that we have

p(t)=\frac{t}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{dp}{dt}(t)+(\text{higher order terms})

(3.15)

and set

r(t):=(\text{higher order terms})=p(t)-\frac{t}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{dp}{dt}(t).

(3.16)

Since
\frac{dp}{dt}(t)
is a tangent vector of
the manifold
S_{\mathbb{R}}
at the point
p(t)\in S_{\mathbb{R}}
, by the proof of Lemma
3.1

we have

\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),\overline{{\rm grad}h(p(t))}\Bigr)=\Bigl\langle\frac{dp}{dt}(t),{\rm grad}h(p(t))\Bigr\rangle=0.

(3.17)

Then by (
3.10
) we obtain

q(t)=\Phi_{p(t)}(p(t))=p(t)-\frac{(r(t),\overline{{\rm grad}h(p(t))})}{(\overline{{\rm grad}h(p(t))},\overline{{\rm grad}h(p(t))})}\cdot\overline{{\rm grad}h(p(t))}

(3.18)

from which the asssertion immediately follows.
If
{\rm dim}S<{\rm dim}X-1=N-1
, we repeat this
argument.
∎

From now on, let us consider also a rational function

f=\frac{P}{Q}:X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(
P,Q\in\Gamma(X;\mathcal{O}_{X})\simeq\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{N}]
,

Q\not=0
) on the smooth algebraic variety

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
and set

U:=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
. Let
S\subset U

be a smooth quasi-affine subvariety of
U
and
denote the restriction
f|_{S}:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

of
f
to it by
g
. Then
for the cotangent vector
d{\rm Re}g(z)\in T^{*}_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}

at a point
z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}

we define its norm
||d{\rm Re}g(z)||\geq 0

to be that of the element
{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(z)\in T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\subset T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
which corresponds to
it by the isomorphism

\psi:T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}
. For
polynomial functions
f:\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
the following condition was first introduced by
Broughton
[
5
]
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
S\subset U
and
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be
as above. Then we say that
g
is tame
at infinity if there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that

\{z\in S\ |\ ||d{\rm Re}g(z)||<\varepsilon,\ ||z||>R\}=\emptyset.

(3.19)

For polynomial functions
f:\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

on
\mathbb{C}^{N}
the following result was essentially obtained in
Némethi-Zaharia
[
52
]
and
[
53
]
.
Here we modify their arguments with the help of
Lemma
3.2
. See also the proof of
Nguyen-Pham-Pham
[
55
, Theorem 3.1]
for a
similar result on complete intersection subvarieties

S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
and in a different formulation
in terms of Rabier’s norm defined in
[
59
]
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
S\subset U
and
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be
as above and assume that
g
is tame
at infinity. We define a subset
M(g)
of
S
by

M(g)=\{z\in S\ |\ d(\delta|_{S})(z)\in\mathbb{R}d{\rm Re}g(z)+\mathbb{R}d{\rm Im}g(z)\}.

(3.20)

Then for any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that

M(g)\cap\{z\in S\ |\ |g(z)-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon,\ ||z||>R\}=\emptyset.

(3.21)

Proof.

Fix a point
z\in S
and recall that
we set
g=f|_{S}
. We denote by

{\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z)\in T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}

the element which corresponds to

d{\rm Re}f(z)\in T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}

by the isomorphism

\phi:T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
by the Cauchy-Riemann equations we have

\displaystyle{\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{\partial{\rm Re}f}{\partial x_{1}}+i\frac{\partial{\rm Re}f}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots\ldots,\frac{\partial{\rm Re}f}{\partial x_{N}}+i\frac{\partial{\rm Re}f}{\partial y_{N}}\Bigr)

(3.22)

\displaystyle=\overline{{\rm grad}f(z)}=\Bigl(\overline{\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{1}}},\ldots\ldots,\overline{\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{N}}}\Bigr),

(3.23)

where we set
z_{i}=x_{i}+iy_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq N
).
Moreover we have

{\rm grad}{\rm Im}f(z)=\Bigl(\frac{\partial{\rm Im}f}{\partial x_{1}}+i\frac{\partial{\rm Im}f}{\partial y_{1}},\ldots\ldots,\frac{\partial{\rm Im}f}{\partial x_{N}}+i\frac{\partial{\rm Im}f}{\partial y_{N}}\Bigr)=i\cdot{\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z).

(3.24)

By Lemma
3.1

this implies that for a point
z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}

such that
||z||\gg 0
the condition

d(\delta|_{S})(z)\in\mathbb{R}d{\rm Re}g(z)+\mathbb{R}d{\rm Im}g(z)

(3.25)

is equivalent to the one that there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that

{\rm grad}(\delta|_{S})(z)=\Phi_{z}(\lambda\cdot{\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z)).

(3.26)

Here we used the fact that if
||z||\gg 0
we have

d(\delta|_{S})(z)\not=0
(see e.g.
[
46
, Corollary 2.8]
).
Since we have
{\rm grad}(\delta|_{S})(z)=\Phi_{z}({\rm grad}\delta(z))=\Phi_{z}(z)
and
\Phi_{z}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linear,
it is also equivalent to the one that there exists
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{*}:=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that

\lambda\cdot\Phi_{z}(z)=\Phi_{z}({\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z))={\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(z).

(3.27)

Now let us prove the assertion by showing a contradiction.
Assume to the contrary that there exists a sequence

z_{n}\in M(g)\subset S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
n=1,2,\ldots
) such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}|g(z_{n})-\tau_{0}|=0,\qquad\lim_{n\to+\infty}||z_{n}||=+\infty.

(3.28)

Then by the curve selection lemma
[
53
, Lemma 4]

of Némethi and Zaharia, there exists
an analytic curve
p(t):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow M(g)\subset S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

such that

\lim_{t\to+0}|g(p(t))-\tau_{0}|=0,\qquad\lim_{t\to+0}||p(t)||=+\infty.

(3.29)

Assume that its expansion at infinity is of the form

p(t)=at^{\alpha}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(a\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\},\alpha<0).

(3.30)

and define
q(t):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
by

q(t)=\Phi_{p(t)}(p(t))\qquad(0<t<\varepsilon).

(3.31)

Then by Lemma
3.2

the expansion at infinity of
q(t)

has the same top term as that of
p(t)
:

q(t)=at^{\alpha}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(a\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\},\alpha<0).

(3.32)

Moreover, by the condition

\lim_{t\to+0}g(p(t))=\tau_{0},

(3.33)

the expansion at infinity
of
g(p(t))
is of the form

g(p(t))=bt^{\beta}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(b\in\mathbb{C}^{*},\beta\geq 0).

(3.34)

Since
g
is tame at infinity, the expansion at infinity
of
{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(p(t)):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}

is of the form

{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(p(t))=ct^{\gamma}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(c\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\},\gamma\leq 0).

(3.35)

Then by the condition
p(t)\in M(g)\subset S

(
0<t<\varepsilon
) and the above argument,
there exists an analytic curve

\lambda(t):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}

such that

\lambda(t)\cdot q(t)=\lambda(t)\cdot\Phi_{p(t)}(p(t))={\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(p(t))\qquad(0<t<\varepsilon).

(3.36)

If the expansion at infinity
of
\lambda(t)
is of the form

\lambda(t)=\lambda_{0}t^{\gamma-\alpha}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{*}),

(3.37)

we have
c=\lambda_{0}a\in\mathbb{C}^{N}\setminus\{0\}
and

{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(p(t))=\lambda_{0}at^{\gamma}+(\text{higher order terms})\qquad(\gamma\leq 0).

(3.38)

On the other hand, for the Hermitian inner product

(\cdot,\cdot)
we have
