\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}g(p(t))

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}f(p(t))=\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),\overline{{\rm grad}f(p(t))}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),{\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(p(t))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),\Phi_{p(t)}({\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(p(t)))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(p(t))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{dp}{dt}(t),\lambda(t)\cdot q(t)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=(\alpha a,\lambda_{0}a)\cdot t^{\alpha-1+\gamma}+(\text{higher order terms}),

where we used (
3.10
) in the fourth equality
(see also the proof of Lemma
3.1
).
Since
(\alpha a,\lambda_{0}a)=\alpha\overline{\lambda_{0}}||a||^{2}\not=0
,

\alpha-1+\gamma<-1
and the left hand side

\frac{d}{dt}g(p(t))
is of order
>-1
in
t

by (
3.34
),
we obtain the desired contradiction. This
completes the proof.
∎

For
r>0
let
B_{r}(0)=\{z\in X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\ |\ ||z||<r\}\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
be the open ball
of radius
r
centered at the origin
0\in X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Then Propositon
3.4

means that if
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
tame at infinity the boundary
\partial B_{r}(0)\cap S
of
B_{r}(0)\cap S
for
r\gg 0

intersects the fibers
g^{-1}(\tau)\subset S

(
|\tau-\tau_{0}|\ll 1
) of
g
transversally.
Now let
K\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
be
an algebraic constructible sheaf
on
X^{{\rm an}}
. Namely we assume that
K
is
adapted to an algebraic stratification of
X^{{\rm an}}=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Then the micro-support

{\rm SS}(K)\subset T^{*}X^{{\rm an}}
of
K
is
a homogeneous Lagrangian subvariety of

(T^{*}X)^{{\rm an}}\simeq T^{*}X^{{\rm an}}
and
there exists an algebraic Whitney
stratification
\mathcal{S}
of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}

such that

{\rm SS}(K)\subset\bigsqcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}T^{*}_{S}X.

(3.39)

Recall that by the Whitney condition the right
hand side is a closed subset in
(T^{*}X)^{{\rm an}}\simeq T^{*}X^{{\rm an}}
.
For
r>0
we define an open subset

U_{r}\subset U
of
U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)
by

U_{r}:=U\cap B_{r}(0)=\{z\in U\ |\ ||z||<r\}\subset U\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}.

(3.40)

Then in order to apply the direct image theorem

[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
for non-proper maps of
Kashiwara and Schapira to

K|_{U}\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(U^{{\rm an}})
and
f:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we shall prove the
following result.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the Whitney
stratification of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}
as above and
assume that for any stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}
in it
such that
S\cap U\not=\emptyset
and

T^{*}_{S}X\subset{\rm SS}(K)

the restriction
f|_{S\cap U}:S\cap U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
of
f
to
S\cap U

is tame at infinity.
Then for any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that we have

N^{*}(U_{r})\cap\Bigl\{\overline{{\rm SS}(K|_{U})+\rho(U\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C})}\Bigr\}\subset T^{*}_{U}U

(3.41)

(for the definition of
N^{*}(U_{r})\subset T^{*}U

see
[
27
, Definition 5.3.6]
) over
the open subset
\{z\in U\ |\ |f(z)-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset U
of
U
for any
r\geq R
,
where
\rho:U\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow T^{*}U
is the natural morphism
associated to
f:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover the same is true even after replacing
N^{*}(U_{r})

by its antipodal set
N^{*}(U_{r})^{{\rm a}}
.

Proof.

Since the subset
\overline{{\rm SS}(K|_{U})+\rho(U\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C})}\subset T^{*}U
of
T^{*}U

is stable by the antipodal map of
T^{*}U
,
it suffices to consider only
N^{*}(U_{r})
.
We prove the assertion by showing a contradiction.
Assume to the contrary that there exists a sequence

z_{n}\in U\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
n=1,2,\ldots
) such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}|f(z_{n})-\tau_{0}|=0,\qquad\lim_{n\to+\infty}||z_{n}||=+\infty

(3.42)

and for the non-zero inner conormal vector

-d\delta(z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
of the real hypersurface

\partial U_{||z_{n}||}\subset U
at
the point
z_{n}\in U
we have

(z_{n},w_{n}):=(z_{n},-d\delta(z_{n}))\in\overline{{\rm SS}(K|_{U})+\rho(U\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C})}\subset T^{*}U

(3.43)

for any
n=1,2,\ldots
. By taking a
subsequence, we may assume that there
exists a stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}
such that

S\cap U\not=\emptyset
and

z_{n}\in S\cap U
for any
n=1,2,\ldots
.
First, let us consider the case where

T^{*}_{S}X\subset{\rm SS}(K)
. Then for
each
n=1,2,\ldots
there exist a
stratum
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
such that

S\subset\overline{S^{\prime}}
and

T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}X\subset{\rm SS}(K)

and sequences
(z_{nm},w_{nm})\in T^{*}_{S^{\prime}\cap U}X
,

\lambda_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
) such that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}z_{nm}=z_{n},\qquad\lim_{m\to+\infty}\bigl(w_{nm}+\lambda_{nm}\cdot df(z_{nm})\bigr)=w_{n}.

(3.44)

Since
f|_{S\cap U}:S\cap U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity
by our assumption, there exists

0<\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
such that
for large enough
n\gg 0
we have
the condition
||d(f|_{S\cap U})(z_{n})||\geq\varepsilon_{0}>0
. On the other hand,
by the Whitney condition of
\mathcal{S}

a subsequence of

w_{nm}/||w_{nm}||\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
,

||w_{nm}||\not=0
)
converges to a point in
(T^{*}_{S}X)_{z_{n}}
.
As
df(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
is holomorphic with respect to

z
and bounded on a neighborhood of the point
z_{n}\in S\cap U
in
U
, these conditions
in together imply that all the sequences

w_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
\lambda_{nm}\cdot df(z_{nm})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,

\lambda_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
)
are bounded. Indeed, if the sequence
w_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
)
is not bounded, by (
3.44
) a subsequence
of
\lambda_{nm}\cdot df(z_{nm})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
)
goes to at infinity of a direction in
(T^{*}_{S}X)_{z_{n}}
.
This contradicts with the condition
||d(f|_{S\cap U})(z_{n})||\geq\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
Hence, by taking their subsequences,
we may assume that all of them converge.
In particular, by the Whitney condition of
\mathcal{S}
we have

\lim_{m\to+\infty}w_{nm}\in(T^{*}_{S}X)_{z_{n}}.

(3.45)

Hence for any
n=1,2,\ldots

there exists
\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

d(\delta|_{S\cap U})(z_{n})=\lambda_{n}\cdot d(f|_{S\cap U})(z_{n})

(3.46)

and we get a contradiction by Proposition

3.4
. Next, let us consider the case where

T^{*}_{S}X
is not contained in
{\rm SS}(K)
.
Also in this case, for
each
n=1,2,\ldots
there exist a
stratum
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
such that

S\subset\overline{S^{\prime}}
and

T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}X\subset{\rm SS}(K)

and sequences
(z_{nm},w_{nm})\in T^{*}_{S^{\prime}\cap U}X
,

\lambda_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
) such that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}z_{nm}=z_{n},\qquad\lim_{m\to+\infty}\bigl(w_{nm}+\lambda_{nm}\cdot df(z_{nm})\bigr)=w_{n}.

(3.47)

By taking their subsequences and using
the Whitney condition of
\mathcal{S}
, we may assume that
the tangent planes
T_{z_{nm}}S^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}
of

S^{\prime}
at
z_{nm}\in S^{\prime}

converge to a linear subspace

\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset T_{z_{n}}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}

such that
T_{z_{n}}S\subset\mathcal{T}_{n}
as

m
tends to
+\infty
. Let

\Phi_{n}:T^{*}_{z_{n}}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{*}_{n}:={\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{T}_{n},\mathbb{C})

(3.48)

be the surjective linear map associated to
the inclusion map
\mathcal{T}_{n}\hookrightarrow T_{z_{n}}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
Then by our assumption for the stratum
S^{\prime}
,
there exists
0<\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
such that
for large enough
n\gg 0
we have
the condition
||\Phi_{n}\bigl(df(z_{n})\bigr)||\geq\varepsilon_{0}>0
.
Similarly to the previous case, we can thus
show that all the sequences

w_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
\lambda_{nm}\cdot df(z_{nm})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,

\lambda_{nm}\in\mathbb{C}
(
m=1,2,\ldots
)
are bounded. By taking subsequences,
we can assume also that all of them converge.
Hence for any
n=1,2,\ldots

there exists
\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\Phi_{n}\bigl(d\delta(z_{n})\bigr)=\lambda_{n}\cdot\Phi_{n}\bigl(df(z_{n})\bigr).

(3.49)

By taking a subsequence of
z_{n}\in S\cap U

(
n=1,2,\ldots
), we may assume that
there exists a stratum
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}

such that
S\subset\overline{S^{\prime}}
,

T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}X\subset{\rm SS}(K)
,
and for any

n=1,2,\ldots
the linear subspace

\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset T_{z_{n}}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
at
z_{n}\in S\cap U

is a limit of some tangent spaces
of
S^{\prime}
.
We fix such
S^{\prime}
once and for all.
Set
l:={\rm dim}S^{\prime}={\rm dim}\mathcal{T}_{n}

(
n=1,2,\ldots
) and let
{\rm Gr}
be the
complex Grassmann manifold consisting of

l
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
Let
A
be a subset of
(S\cap U)\times{\rm Gr}

consisting of pairs
(z,\mathcal{T})\in(S\cap U)\times{\rm Gr}

such that there exists
a sequence
z_{m}\in S^{\prime}\cap U
(
m=1,2,\ldots
)
in the stratum
S^{\prime}
such that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}z_{m}=z,\qquad\lim_{m\to+\infty}T_{z_{m}}S^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}

(3.50)

and for the surjective linear map

\Phi_{(z,\mathcal{T})}:T^{*}_{z}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{*}:={\rm Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{T},\mathbb{C})

(3.51)

associated to the inclusion map
\mathcal{T}\hookrightarrow T_{z}X\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
we have

\Phi_{(z,\mathcal{T})}\bigl(d\delta(z)\bigr)=\lambda\cdot\Phi_{(z,\mathcal{T})}\bigl(df(z)\bigr)

(3.52)

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
we can easily show that
A\subset(S\cap U)\times{\rm Gr}\subset X\times{\rm Gr}
is a semi-analytic
subset of
X\times{\rm Gr}
.
In the above argument, we obtained a sequence

(z_{n},\mathcal{T}_{n})\in A\subset(S\cap U)\times{\rm Gr}

in
A
such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}|f(z_{n})-\tau_{0}|=0,\qquad\lim_{n\to+\infty}||z_{n}||=+\infty.

(3.53)

Since the complex Grassmann manifold
{\rm Gr}

is covered by finitely many open subsets
isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{l(N-l)}
, we can apply
the curve selection lemma
[
53
, Lemma 4]

of Némethi and Zaharia to find
an analytic curve

q(t)=(p(t),\mathcal{T}(t)):(0,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow A\subset(S\cap U)\times{\rm Gr}

(3.54)

in
A
satisfying the conditions

\lim_{t\to+0}|f(p(t))-\tau_{0}|=0,\qquad\lim_{t\to+0}||p(t)||=+\infty.

(3.55)

Then we obtain a contradiction as in
the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
More precisely, by taking a family of conormal
vectors of the planes
\mathcal{T}(t)\subset\mathbb{C}^{N}

which depend analytically on

t\in(0,\varepsilon)
, we obtain a result
similar to Lemma
3.2
and
can apply the proof of Proposition
3.4

to our situation.
This completes the proof.
∎

By the proof of Proposition
3.5
,
we obtain also the followng simple consequence.

Lemma 3.6
.

In the situation of Proposition
3.5
,
for any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that we have

df(z)\notin{\rm SS}(K|_{U})

(3.56)

for any
z\in U
such that
||z||\geq R

and
|f(z)-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon
.

First, let us consider the
special case where
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
. Note that this condition is
satisfied for example if
f=\frac{P}{Q}
is a polynomial
i.e.
Q=1
or
P=1
.

Theorem 3.7
.

In the situation of Proposition
3.5
,
assume also that
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
.
Then we have the following results.

(i)

For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that for the
inclusion maps
i_{r}:B_{r}(0)\hookrightarrow X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

(
r>R
) we have isomorphisms

{\rm R}f_{!}(i_{r})_{!}(i_{r}^{-1}K)\simeq{\rm R}f_{!}K_{B_{r}(0)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}f_{!}K

(3.57)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, if we assume also that

\{z\in f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\ |\ df(z)\in{\rm SS}(K)\}\subset f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap{\rm supp}(K)

(3.58)

is a finite subset of
f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap{\rm supp}(K)

and let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}
be the points in it,
then for the vanishing cycle

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}K)\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\{\tau_{0}\})
of
{\rm R}f_{!}K

along the function
\tau-\tau_{0}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

we have an isomorphism

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}K)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K)_{p_{i}}.

(3.59)

If we assume moreover that

K[N]\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
is a perverse sheaf,
then we have also a concentration

H^{j}\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}K)\simeq 0\qquad(j\not=N-1).

(3.60)

Proof.

By combining
[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
with
Proposition
3.5

we obtain the isomorphism in (i).
Let us prove (ii). For
R\gg 0
such that

p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\in B_{R}(0)
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
in (i)
let us fix
r>R
.
Then the morphism
f
being proper on
the support of

(i_{r})_{!}(i_{r}^{-1}K)\simeq K_{B_{r}(0)},

(3.61)

by (i) we obtain an isomorphism

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}K)\simeq\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{*}K_{B_{r}(0)})\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(f^{-1}(\tau_{0});\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)})).

(3.62)

Moreover by our assumption, the
complex hypersurface
f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is smooth on a neighborhood of

\bigl(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\setminus\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\}\bigr)\cap{\rm supp}(K)

(3.63)

in
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Then by

[
27
, Propositon 8.6.3]
we see that
the support of
\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)})\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(f^{-1}(\tau_{0}))
is contained in the set

\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\}\sqcup\bigl(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap\partial B_{r}(0)\bigr).

(3.64)

Now let us take
r^{\prime}>R
such that

r>r^{\prime}>R
. Then by the proof of

[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
there exists an
isomorphism

{\rm R}f_{!}K_{B_{r^{\prime}}(0)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}f_{!}K_{B_{r}(0)}

(3.65)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.
In other words, we have a vanishing

{\rm R}f_{!}(K_{B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0)})\simeq 0

(3.66)

there. This implies that we have

{\rm R}\Gamma(f^{-1}(\tau_{0});\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0)}))\simeq 0.

(3.67)

Since the support of
\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0)})\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(f^{-1}(\tau_{0}))
is contained in the set

\bigl(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap\partial B_{r}(0)\bigr)\sqcup\bigl(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap\partial B_{r^{\prime}}(0)\bigr)

(3.68)

and hence the object
{\rm R}\Gamma(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap\partial B_{r}(0);\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)}))
is a direct
summand of the one
{\rm R}\Gamma(f^{-1}(\tau_{0});\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0)}))\simeq 0
, we obtain also a vanishing

{\rm R}\Gamma(f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap\partial B_{r}(0);\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K_{B_{r}(0)}))\simeq 0.

(3.69)

From this, the first assertion of (ii) immediately follows.
To obtain the last one, it suffices to use
the t-exactness of the functor

\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(\cdot)[-1]
.
∎

Similarly, by Proposition
3.5
and

[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
we obtain
the following result.

Theorem 3.8
.

In the situation of Proposition
3.5
,
assume also that
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
.
Then we have the following results.

(i)

For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that for the
inclusion maps
i_{r}:B_{r}(0)\hookrightarrow X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

(
r>R
) we have isomorphisms

{\rm R}f_{*}{\rm R}(i_{r})_{*}(i_{r}^{-1}K)\simeq{\rm R}f_{*}K_{B_{r}(0)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}f_{*}K

(3.70)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, if we assume also that

\{z\in f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\ |\ df(z)\in{\rm SS}(K)\}\subset f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap{\rm supp}(K)

(3.71)

is a finite subset of
f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap{\rm supp}(K)

and let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}
be the points in it,
then for the vanishing cycle

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{*}K)\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\{\tau_{0}\})
of
{\rm R}f_{*}K

along the function
\tau-\tau_{0}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

we have an isomorphism

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{*}K)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K)_{p_{i}}.

(3.72)

If we assume moreover that

K[N]\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
is a perverse sheaf,
then we have also a concentration

H^{j}\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{*}K)\simeq 0\qquad(j\not=N-1).

(3.73)

Next, let us consider the problem in the general case i.e.
in the absence of the condition
I(f)=P^{-1}(0)\cap Q^{-1}(0)=\emptyset
. For this purpose, we regard
the projection
\tau:X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
as a holomorphic function on

X\times\mathbb{C}
and denote it by
h
.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
S\subset X\times\mathbb{C}
be a smooth quasi-affine
subvariety of
X\times\mathbb{C}
.
Then we say that the restriction
h|_{S}
of
the function
h
to
S
is relatively tame
at infinity for the projection
X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
if there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that

\{(z,\tau)\in S\ |\ ||d{\rm Re}(h|_{S})(z,\tau)||<\varepsilon,\ ||z||>R\}=\emptyset.

(3.74)

For the rational function
f=\frac{P}{Q}:U=X\setminus Q^{-1}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
the algebraic constructible sheaf

K\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
by using the (not necessarily)
closed embedding
i_{f}:U\longrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}

(
z\longmapsto(z,f(z))
) we set

L:=i_{f!}(K|_{U})\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}}\times\mathbb{C}).

(3.75)

Then there exists an algebraic Whitney
stratification
\mathcal{S}
of
X\times\mathbb{C}
such that

{\rm SS}(L)\subset\bigsqcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}}T^{*}_{S}(X\times\mathbb{C}).

(3.76)

Moreover we may assume also that for
\mathcal{S}
and a Whitney
stratification
\mathcal{S}_{0}
of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

the projection
X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
is a stratified fiber
bundle as in the assertion of the theorem in

[
15
, page 43]
. Indeed, after extending

L
to a constructible sheaf on
X\times{\mathbb{P}}

we can apply this theorem to the
proper morphism
X\times{\mathbb{P}}\longrightarrow X
(
(z,\tau)\longmapsto z
).
Let us fix such Whitney stratifications

\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. Then we shall say that a stratum

S\in\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{S}
is horizontal if
for its projection
S_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
to
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

we have
{\rm dim}S_{0}={\rm dim}S
i.e.
the surjective
submersion
S\longrightarrow S_{0}
induced
by the projection

X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X

is a finite covering. Obviously, if

S\in\mathcal{S}
is not horizontal then
for its projection
S_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
to
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

we have
{\rm dim}S_{0}={\rm dim}S-1
and hence
the restriction
h|_{S}
of
the function
h
to
S
is relatively tame
at infinity for the projection
X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
. Namely, our relative
tameness at infinity of
h|_{S}
is a
constraint only for horizontal strata
S\in\mathcal{S}
.
Moreover it is easy to see that if for
a horizontal stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{S}

there exists a holomorphic function
g

on its projection
S_{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
to
X

such that

S=\{(z,g(z))\ |\ z\in S_{0}\}\subset X\times\mathbb{C}

(3.77)

then the relative
tameness at infinity of
h|_{S}
is equivalent
to the tameness at infinity of
g:S_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
in Definition
3.3
.
The proof of the following proposition is very similar
to that of Proposition
3.5
and
we omit it.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the Whitney
stratification of
X\times\mathbb{C}
as above and
assume that for any stratum
S\in\mathcal{S}
in it
such that

T^{*}_{S}(X\times\mathbb{C})\subset{\rm SS}(L)

the restriction
h|_{S}:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
of the function
h
to

S\subset X\times\mathbb{C}

is relatively tame at infinity for
the projection
X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow X
.
Then for any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that we have

N^{*}(B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C})\cap\Bigl\{\overline{{\rm SS}(L)+\rho\bigl((X\times\mathbb{C})\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\bigr)}\Bigr\}\subset T^{*}_{(X\times\mathbb{C})}(X\times\mathbb{C})

(3.78)

over
the open subset
\{(z,\tau)\in X\times\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset X\times\mathbb{C}
of
X\times\mathbb{C}
for any
r\geq R
,
where
\rho:(X\times\mathbb{C})\times_{\mathbb{C}}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow T^{*}(X\times\mathbb{C})
is the closed embedding
associated to the projection
h:X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover the same is true even after replacing

N^{*}(B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C})

by its antipodal set
N^{*}(B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C})^{{\rm a}}
.

Theorem 3.11
.

In the situation of Proposition
3.10

we have the following results.

(i)

For any
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}

there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that for the
inclusion maps
i_{r}:U_{r}=U\cap B_{r}(0)\hookrightarrow U

(
r>R
) we have isomorphisms

{\rm R}f_{!}(i_{r})_{!}(i_{r})^{-1}(K|_{U})\simeq{\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U})_{U_{r}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U})

(3.79)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

For
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
we assume also that

\{z\in f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\ |\ df(z)\in{\rm SS}(K)\}\subset f^{-1}(\tau_{0})\cap{\rm supp}(K|_{U})

(3.80)

and

\{z\in I(f)\ |\ \phi_{f-\tau_{0}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(K)_{z}\not=0\}\subset I(f)\cap{\rm supp}(K)

(3.81)

are finite subsets of
Z={\rm supp}(K)

and denote them by
\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\}

and
\{q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}\}
respectively.
Then for the vanishing cycle

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U}))\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\{\tau_{0}\})
of
{\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U})

along the function
\tau-\tau_{0}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

we have an isomorphism

\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U}))\simeq\Bigl\{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(K)_{p_{i}}\Bigr\}\oplus\Bigl\{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(K)_{q_{i}}\Bigr\}.

(3.82)

If we assume moreover that

K[N]\in{\bf D}_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{b}}(X^{{\rm an}})
is a perverse sheaf,
then we have also a concentration

H^{j}\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U}))\simeq 0\qquad(j\not=N-1).

(3.83)

Proof.

By
[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
and
Proposition
3.10

there exist
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
such that for the
inclusion maps
j_{r}:B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow X\times\mathbb{C}

(
r>R
) we have isomorphisms

{\rm R}h_{!}(j_{r})_{!}(j_{r})^{-1}L\simeq{\rm R}h_{!}L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}h_{!}L

(3.84)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.
Moreover we have
{\rm R}h_{!}L\simeq{\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U})

and for any
r>0
there exist an isomorphism

{\rm R}h_{!}L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}\simeq{\rm R}h_{!}\Bigl(i_{f!}(K|_{U})\otimes\mathbb{C}_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}\Bigr)\simeq{\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U})_{U_{r}}.

(3.85)

We thus obtain the isomorphism in (i).
Let us prove (ii). Let
R\gg 0
and

0<\varepsilon\ll 1
be as in (i). Here
we take
R>0
so that also the condition

p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k},q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}\in B_{R}(0)
is satisfied. Then by the
proof of (i), for any
r>R
we obtain isomorphisms

\displaystyle\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}f_{!}(K|_{U}))\simeq\phi_{\tau-\tau_{0}}({\rm R}h_{!}L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\Gamma(X\times\{\tau_{0}\};\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}})),

where in the second isomorphism we used the fact
that the morphism
h:X\times\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

is proper on the support of
L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}
.
On the other hand, there exist also isomorphisms

\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L)\simeq\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(i_{f!}(K|_{U}))\simeq\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(K)

(3.86)

under the natural identification
h^{-1}(\tau_{0})=X\times\{\tau_{0}\}\simeq X
.
Similarly, we have isomorphisms

\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}})\simeq\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(i_{f!}(K_{B_{r}(0)}|_{U}))\simeq\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(K_{B_{r}(0)}).

(3.87)

Then together with our assumptions, this implies
that for any
r>R
the support of the vanishing cycle

\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}})
is
contained in the set

\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\}\sqcup\{q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{l}\}\sqcup\partial B_{r}(0).

(3.88)

Now let us take
r,r^{\prime}>R
such that

r>r^{\prime}
. Then by the proof of

[
26
, Theorem 4.4.1]
there exists an
isomorphism

{\rm R}h_{!}L_{B_{r^{\prime}}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}h_{!}L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}

(3.89)

on the open subset
\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\ |\ |\tau-\tau_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
.
In other words, we have a vanishing

{\rm R}h_{!}L_{(B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0))\times\mathbb{C}}\simeq 0

(3.90)

there. This implies that we have

{\rm R}\Gamma(X\times\{\tau_{0}\};\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{(B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0))\times\mathbb{C}}))\simeq 0.

(3.91)

Since the support of

\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{(B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0))\times\mathbb{C}})

is contained in the set

\partial B_{r}(0)\sqcup\partial B_{r^{\prime}}(0)

(3.92)

and hence the object
{\rm R}\Gamma(\partial B_{r}(0);\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}))
is a direct
summand of the one
{\rm R}\Gamma(X\times\{\tau_{0}\};\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{(B_{r}(0)\setminus B_{r^{\prime}}(0))\times\mathbb{C}}))\simeq 0
, we obtain also a vanishing

{\rm R}\Gamma(\partial B_{r}(0);\phi_{h-\tau_{0}}(L_{B_{r}(0)\times\mathbb{C}}))\simeq 0.

(3.93)

From this, the first assertion of (ii) immediately follows.
The second one follows from the fact that
the functors
\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}(\cdot)[-1]
and

\phi_{f-\tau_{0}}^{{\rm mero,c}}(\cdot)[-1]
preserve
the perversity. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, to end this section, we shall introduce
some geometric consequences of Proposition
3.4

which generalize the main results of Broughton
[
5
]
.
Let
f\in\mathbb{C}[z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{N}]
be a polynomial
on
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
and
S\subset X
a smooth subvariety of

X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Let
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the
restriction of
f:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
to
S\subset X
.
Then it is well-known that there exists a finite
subset
B\subset\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}
such that the
restriction
g^{-1}(\mathbb{C}\setminus B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}\setminus B

of
g
is a
{\rm C}^{\infty}
-locally trivial fibration.
We denote by
B_{g}
the smallest finite subset of

\mathbb{C}
satisfying this property and call it the
bifurcation set of
g
. By this definition, it is
clear that the set
\Sigma_{g}:=g({\rm Sing}g)\subset\mathbb{C}
of the critical values of
g
is contained in

B_{g}
. Note that as was observed in
[
5
]

there are a lot of polynomial maps
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

such that
B_{g}\not=\Sigma_{g}
. Nevertheless,
by Proposition
3.4
we can easily show
the following result as in the proof of

[
52
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 3.12
.

(cf. Nguyen-Pham-Pham
[
55
, Theorem 3.1]
)
Assume that
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity.
Then we have
B_{g}=\Sigma_{g}
.

By Theorem
3.8
, we obtain also the
analogues of the results in Sabbah
[
62
, Section 8]
.
Here, instead of Sabbah’s cohomological tameness,
we assume our topological one in Definition
3.3
.
In particular, we obtain the following results.

Lemma 3.13
.

(cf. Sabbah
[
62
, Lemma 8.5]
)
Assume that
n:={\rm dim}S-1\geq 1
and

g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity.
Then we have

H^{j}{\rm R}g_{*}\mathbb{C}_{S}\simeq 0\qquad(j\notin[0,n]).

(3.94)

Moreover for any
1\leq j\leq n-1
the direct image
sheaf
H^{j}{\rm R}g_{*}\mathbb{C}_{S}
is a constant sheaf on

\mathbb{C}
of rank
{\rm dim}H^{j}(S;\mathbb{C})
.

As is the proof of
[
62
, Lemma 8.5]
,
in the situation of Lemma
3.13
we
see that for any point
c\in\mathbb{C}

we have isomorphisms

H^{j}({\rm R}g_{*}\mathbb{C}_{S})_{c}\simeq H^{j}(g^{-1}(c);\mathbb{C})\qquad(j\in\mathbb{Z}).

(3.95)

We thus obtain the following very simple
consequence of Lemma
3.13
.

Corollary 3.14
.

In the situation of Lemma
3.13
,
for the finite subset
B_{g}=\Sigma_{g}\subset\mathbb{C}

of
\mathbb{C}
and a base point
c\in\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{g}

the monodromy representations

\rho_{g,c,j}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{g};c)\longrightarrow{\rm Aut}\Bigl(H^{j}(g^{-1}(c);\mathbb{C})\Bigr)\qquad(0\leq j\leq n-1)

(3.96)

are trivial.

By Corollaly
3.14
the monodromy representation

\rho_{g,c,n}:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{g};c)\longrightarrow{\rm Aut}\Bigl(H^{n}(g^{-1}(c);\mathbb{C})\Bigr)

(3.97)

in the top degree
n={\rm dim}g^{-1}(c)

is the only non-trivial one and we can calculate
its eigenvalues by the theory of monodromy zeta
functions (see e.g.
[
66
, Section 2]
).
For a polynomial map
h:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
on
S

we denote by
\mu(h)
the sum of the Milnor numbers of

h
. If the set
{\rm Sing}h\subset S
of the
critical points of
h
is not finite, we set

\mu(h):=+\infty
.

Lemma 3.15
.

(cf. Sabbah
[
62
, Remark just after Lemma 8.5]
)
In the situation of Lemma
3.13
, the
generic rank of the constructible sheaf

H^{n}{\rm R}g_{*}\mathbb{C}_{S}
on
\mathbb{C}
is equal to
the number

\mu(g)+{{\rm dim}}H^{n}(S;\mathbb{C})-{{\rm dim}}H^{n+1}(S;\mathbb{C}).

(3.98)

Moreover, by the proof of
[
62
, Lemma 8.5]

and Lemma
3.15
we obtain the following result.

Corollary 3.16
.

In the situation of Lemma
3.15
,
for a point
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}

we denote by
\mu_{c_{0}}
the sum of the Milnor
numbers of the hypersurface
g^{-1}(c_{0})\subset S

and set
\mu(g)^{\prime}:=\mu(g)+{{\rm dim}}H^{n}(S;\mathbb{C})-{{\rm dim}}H^{n+1}(S;\mathbb{C})
.
Then for any point
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
we have

{\rm dim}H^{n}(g^{-1}(c_{0});\mathbb{C})=\begin{cases}\mu(g)^{\prime}&(c_{0}\notin B_{g}=\Sigma_{g})\\
\\
\mu(g)^{\prime}-\mu_{c_{0}}&(c_{0}\in B_{g}=\Sigma_{g}).\end{cases}

(3.99)

Now let
Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{w}
be the dual vector space of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}_{z}

and consider the Lagrangian subvariety

\Lambda^{g}:=\{(z,dg(z))\ |\ z\in S\}\qquad\subset T^{*}S

(3.100)

of
T^{*}S
and the natural morphisms

T^{*}X\overset{\varpi}{\longleftarrow}S\times_{X}T^{*}X\overset{\rho}{\longrightarrow}T^{*}S

(3.101)

associated to the inclusion map

S\hookrightarrow X
. Then
\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\subset T^{*}X
is a Lagrangian subvariety of
T^{*}X
.
For a point
w=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{N})\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
we define a linear perturbation

g^{(w)}:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
of
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

g^{(w)}(z):=g(z)-\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}w_{j}z_{j}\qquad(z\in S\subset X).

(3.102)

Lemma 3.17
.

The polynomial map

g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity
if and only if the restriction
\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\longrightarrow Y
of the projection
T^{*}X\simeq X\times Y\longrightarrow Y
to
\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\subset T^{*}X

is proper over a sufficiently small open ball

B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}

(0<\varepsilon\ll 1)
centered at the origin

0\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

Proof.

First note that the fiber of the morphism

\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\longrightarrow Y
at a point
w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is
naturally identified with the set

{\rm Sing}g^{(w)}=\{z\in S\ |\ dg^{(w)}(z)=0\}\qquad\subset S.

(3.103)

Then the last condition in the lemma is equivalent to
the one that there exist
R\gg 0
and
0<\varepsilon\ll 1

such that

\{z\in S\ |\ dg^{(w)}(z)=0,||z||>R\}=\emptyset

(3.104)

for any
w\in B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
By the Cauchy-Riemann equation, for any
z\in S
and

w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
we have also an equivalence

dg^{(w)}(z)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ {\rm grad}{\rm Re}g^{(w)}(z)=0.

(3.105)

Moreover, for the surjective
\mathbb{C}
-linear map

\Phi_{z}:T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}S}
in Lemma
3.1
,
by (
3.22
) we obtain an equality

{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g^{(w)}(z)={\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(z)-\Phi_{z}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{w_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{w_{N}}\end{array}\right).

(3.106)

Since by the proof of Lemma
3.1
the
\mathbb{C}
-linear map

\Phi_{z}
is the orthogonal projection to

T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}S}
with respect to
the Hermitian metric of
T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
we see that the open subset

\Bigl\{-\Phi_{z}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{w_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{w_{N}}\end{array}\right)\ |\ w\in B_{\varepsilon}(0)\Bigr\}\quad\subset T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}

(3.107)

is the
\varepsilon
-ball in

T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{{\rm dim}S}

centered at the origin. From this
we immediately obtain the assertion.
∎

By the proof of Lemma
3.17
and the
algebraicity of
\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\subset T^{*}X
we can easily see that generic
linear perturbations

g^{(w)}
(
w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
)
of
g
are tame at infinity. More precisely,
we have the following result.

Lemma 3.18
.

Let
\Omega\subset Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
be the maximal Zariski open
subset of
Y
such that the base change of
the morphism

\varpi\rho^{-1}\Lambda^{g}\longrightarrow Y
by the
inclusion map
\Omega\hookrightarrow Y
is a
(possibly ramified) finite covering.
Then for
w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
the linear perturbation

g^{(w)}
of
g
is tame at infinity if and only if

w\in\Omega
.

As in
[
5
, Proposition 3.1]
we obtain also the
following characterization of the tameness
at infinity of
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

The polynomial map
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
tame at infinity if and only if
\mu(g)<+\infty
and

\mu(g)=\mu(g^{(w)})
for all sufficiently small

w\in Y=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

From now on, we shall introduce our results on the
bouquet decompositions of the fibers

h^{-1}(c)\subset S

(c\in\mathbb{C})
of
some tame polynomial maps
h:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
For this purpose, first we recall the following
fundamental theorem due to Broughton
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
.
For a polynomial map
h:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
having
only isolated singular points and a point
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}

we denote by
\mu_{c_{0}}
the sum of the Milnor
numbers of the hypersurface
h^{-1}(c_{0})\subset S
and
set
\mu(h,c_{0}):=\mu(h)-\mu_{c_{0}}
.

Theorem 3.20
.

(Broughton
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
)
Assume that a polynomial map

h:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity.
Then for any point
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
its fiber

h^{-1}(c_{0})\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
of
h

has the homotopy type of a bouquet

S^{N-1}\vee\cdots\vee S^{N-1}
of
some
(N-1)
-dimensional spheres
S^{N-1}
.
Moreover the number of the spheres
S^{N-1}
in
the bouquet decomposition is equal to
\mu(h,c_{0})
.

Recall that this beautiful result was the starting
point of the intensive activities in the study of
the monodromies at infinity of polynomial maps

h:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
(see
e.g.
[
66
, Sections 4 and 7]
for
the details). Motivated by it,
we introduce the following class of
subvarieties of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
.

Definition 3.21
.

We say that a smooth complete intersection subvariety

Z\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a bouquet variety if it
has the homotopy type of a bouquet of
some spheres of dimension
{\rm dim}Z
.

Note that if a polynomial map

h:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity
then by Theorem
3.20
generic fibers of

h
are bouquet varieties.

Theorem 3.22
.

Assume that
n:={\rm dim}S-1\geq 1
, the smooth subvariety

S\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
is a bouquet variety and the polynomial map

g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is tame at infinity.
Denote by
\mu_{S}
the number of the spheres
S^{n+1}
in
the bouquet decomposition of
S
.
Then for any
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
its fiber
g^{-1}(c_{0})\subset S
of
g

has the homotopy type of a bouquet

S^{n}\vee\cdots\vee S^{n}
of
some
n
-dimensional spheres
S^{n}
.
Moreover the number of the spheres
S^{n}
in
the bouquet decomposition is equal to
\mu(g,c_{0})-\mu_{S}
.

Proof.

With Proposition
3.4
and its consequences
above at hands, the proof is very similar to
that of
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
and for it we
use a Morse theory for the Morse function

\varphi(z):=|g(z)-c_{0}|^{2}

(z\in S_{\mathbb{R}})
on
S_{\mathbb{R}}
.
First of all, by Proposition
3.4
for a
sufficiently small
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
the
level set
\{z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ \varphi(z)<\varepsilon\}\subset S_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\varphi
is homotopic to
the fiber
g^{-1}(c_{0})
. Moreover by the Cauchy-Riemann equation
we can easily see that a point
z_{0}\in S\setminus g^{-1}(c_{0})

is a critical point of the Morse function

\varphi(z)=|g(z)-c_{0}|^{2}=\exp\Bigl(2{\rm Re}\log(g(z)-c_{0})\Bigr)

(3.108)

if and only if it is a critical point of
g
.
As in the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
,
by slightly perturbing
g:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

we may assume that any critical point

z_{0}\in S\setminus g^{-1}(c_{0})
of
g
is
non-degenerate i.e. of complex Morse type. Then we can
easily show that any critical point

z_{0}\in S\setminus g^{-1}(c_{0})
of

\varphi
is non-degenerate and has the Morse index

{\rm dim}S=n+1
. This would then imply that for a
sufficiently large
t\gg 0
the
level set
\{z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ \varphi(z)<t\}\subset S_{\mathbb{R}}
of
\varphi
has the homotopy
type of the CW complex obtained by attaching
some
(n+1)
-dimensional cells to
g^{-1}(c_{0})
and
the number of such cells is equal to
\mu(g,c_{0})=\mu(g)-\mu_{c_{0}}
.
However, to justify such a Morse-theoretical argument
on the “non-compact” manifold
S_{\mathbb{R}}
we have to
show that each integral curve of the gradient vector
field
{\rm grad}\varphi/||{\rm grad}\varphi||^{2}

does not go to infinity in a finite time
(see the last part of the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]

and that of Milnor
[
46
, Theorem 2.10]
).
For this purpose, it suffices to show that
for some
R\gg 0
the function
||{\rm grad}\varphi||^{-1}

is bounded on the set
\{z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ ||z||\geq R,\varphi(z)\geq\varepsilon\}
.
First, we define a function
\widetilde{\varphi}:X_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
on
X_{\mathbb{R}}
by

\widetilde{\varphi}(z):=|f(z)-c_{0}|^{2}\qquad(z\in X_{\mathbb{R}}).

(3.109)

Then our Morse function
\varphi:S_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

is the restriction of
\widetilde{\varphi}
to

S_{\mathbb{R}}\subset X_{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover, for any point

z\in X_{\mathbb{R}}
we can easily show an equality

{\rm grad}\widetilde{\varphi}(z)=2(f(z)-c_{0})\cdot\overline{{\rm grad}f(z)}

(3.110)

in
T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Recall that by (
3.22
)
we also have

\overline{{\rm grad}f(z)}={\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z).

(3.111)

Then by Lemma
3.1
, for any point

z\in S_{\mathbb{R}}
there exists a surjective

\mathbb{C}
-linear map

\Phi_{z}:T_{z}X_{\mathbb{R}}\longrightarrow T_{z}S_{\mathbb{R}}

such that

\displaystyle||{\rm grad}\varphi||^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle{\rm grad}\varphi(z),\overline{{\rm grad}\varphi(z)}\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\Bigl({\rm grad}\varphi(z),{\rm grad}\varphi(z)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\Phi_{z}({\rm grad}\widetilde{\varphi}(z)),\Phi_{z}({\rm grad}\widetilde{\varphi}(z))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=4|g(z)-c_{0}|^{2}\cdot||\Phi_{z}({\rm grad}{\rm Re}f(z))||^{2}

\displaystyle=4|g(z)-c_{0}|^{2}\cdot||{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g(z)||^{2}.

Since
g
is tame at inifinity and hence the function

||{\rm grad}{\rm Re}g||=||d{\rm Re}g||\geq 0
on

S_{\mathbb{R}}
is bounded away from
0
at infinity, now
the desired boundedness of
||{\rm grad}\varphi||^{-1}

immediately follows.
Then as in the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
, by
the homotopy exact sequence associated to
the pair
(S,g^{-1}(c_{0}))
of topological spaces we obtain

\pi_{j}(g^{-1}(c_{0}))\simeq 0\qquad(j<n={\rm dim}g^{-1}(c_{0})).

(3.112)

Note also that by the
Andreotti-Frankel theorem in
[
1
]
and Hamm’s
one in
[
19
]
the complete intersection subvariety

g^{-1}(c_{0})\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
has the
homotopy type of a CW complex of
dimension
\leq n={\rm dim}g^{-1}(c_{0})
.
Then we can apply the argument in the proof of Milnor

[
46
, Theorem 6.5]
to

g^{-1}(c_{0})
to show that its (top-dimensional)
homology group
H_{n}(g^{-1}(c_{0}))
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module
and the first assertion immediately
follows from Whitehead’s theorem as in
the proof of
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
. We
can also show the second assertion by Corollary
3.16
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Note that in
[
68
, Theorem 4.6 and Corollary 4.7]

Tibar also obtained a result similar to the first
part of Theorem
3.22
under a different condition
at infinity in an appropriately chosen compactification of
S
.
For
1\leq k\leq N
we define a decreasing sequence

S_{0}:=X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\supset S_{1}\supset S_{2}\supset\cdots\cdots\supset S_{k}

(3.113)

of smooth complete intersection subvarieties
of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
such that
{\rm dim}S_{i}=N-i

(0\leq i\leq k)
as follows. First we take a
tame polynomial
g_{0}:S_{0}=X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

and a point
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{g_{0}}
and set

S_{1}:=g_{0}^{-1}(c_{0})\subset S_{0}
. Next we
repeat this construction and define

S_{i}
for
i\geq 2
recursively. Namely, for
each
1\leq i\leq k-1
after defining
S_{i}
we
take a tame polynomial
g_{i}:S_{i}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

and a point
c_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{g_{i}}
and set

S_{i+1}:=g_{i}^{-1}(c_{i})\subset S_{i}
. Then by
Theorem
3.22
we see that

S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k}
are bouquet varieties.
If for each
0\leq i\leq k
we take a
polynomial map
f_{i}:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

such that
f_{i}|_{S_{i}}=g_{i}
and
set
h_{i}:=f_{i}-c_{i}:X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
,
then we obtain
a smooth complete intersection subvariety

S_{k}=\{z\in X=\mathbb{C}^{N}\ |\ h_{1}(z)=h_{2}(z)=\cdots=h_{k}(z)=0\}\qquad\subset X=\mathbb{C}^{N}

(3.114)

of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
which has the homotopy type of a bouquet

S^{N-k}\vee\cdots\vee S^{N-k}
of
some
(N-k)
-dimensional spheres
S^{N-k}
.
Moreover, by Lemmas
3.17
and
3.18

we can explicitly choose such polynomials

h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{k}
.
This explains the reason why
the results on the monodromies at infinity of
the polynomial maps on the complete intersection
subvarieties of
X=\mathbb{C}^{N}
studied in
Esterov-Takeuchi
[
13
, Section 6]
and
Matsui-Takeuchi
[
44
, Section 5]
are very similar
to the ones in the local case of
Hamm
[
18
]
, Esterov-Takeuchi
[
13
, Sections 4 and 5]
,
Matsui-Takeuchi
[
43
, Theorem 3.12]

and Oka
[
58
]
. Indeed, by Lemma
3.17
we can
easily show that in the Newton non-degenerate
setting of
[
13
, Section 6]
and
[
44
, Section 5]

the generic fibers admit a bouquet decomposition
(under some weak assumptions).

4
An Overview on Ind-sheaves and the Irregular

Riemann-Hilbert Correspondence

In this section, we briefly recall some basic notions
and results which will be used in this paper.
We assume here that the reader is familiar with the
theory of sheaves and functors in the framework of
derived categories. For them we follow the terminologies
in
[
27
]
etc. For a topological space
M

denote by
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})
the derived category
consisting of bounded
complexes of sheaves of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces on it.

4.1
Ind-sheaves

We recall some basic notions
and results on ind-sheaves. References are made to
Kashiwara-Schapira
[
29
]
and
[
31
]
.
Let
M
be a good topological space (which is locally compact,
Hausdorff, countable at infinity and has finite soft dimension).
We denote by
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathbb{C}_{M})
the abelian category of sheaves
of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces on it and by
{\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}

that of ind-sheaves. Then there exists a
natural exact embedding
\iota_{M}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}

of categories. We sometimes omit it.
It has an exact left adjoint
\alpha_{M}
,
that has in turn an exact fully faithful
left adjoint functor
\beta_{M}
:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.93825pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.93825pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.72583pt\raise 9.31526pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.93825pt\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.93839pt\raise-10.41664pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta_{M}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.93825pt\raise-4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 84.93825pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.6062pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00417pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha_{M}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.93825pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

(4.1)

The category
{\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}
does not have enough injectives.
Nevertheless, we can construct the derived category
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})

for ind-sheaves and the Grothendieck six operations among them.
We denote by
\otimes
and
{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom
the operations
of tensor products and internal homs respectively.
If
f:M\to N
be a continuous map, we denote
by
f^{-1},{\rm R}f_{\ast},f^{!}
and
{\rm R}f_{!!}
the
operations of inverse images,
direct images, proper inverse images and proper direct images
respectively.
We set also
{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om:=\alpha_{M}\circ{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom
.
We thus obtain the functors

\displaystyle\iota_{M}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle\alpha_{M}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle\beta_{M}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}f_{\ast}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N}),

\displaystyle f^{-1}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}f_{!!}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N}),

\displaystyle f^{!}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}).

Note that
(f^{-1},{\rm R}f_{\ast})
and

({\rm R}f_{!!},f^{!})
are pairs of adjoint functors.
We may summarize the commutativity of the various functors
we have introduced in the table below.
Here,
``\circ"
means that the functors commute,
and
``\times"
they do not.

\otimes

f^{-1}

{\rm R}f_{\ast}

f^{!}

{\rm R}f_{!!}

\underset{}{\varinjlim}

\varprojlim

\underset{}{\iota}

\circ

\circ

\circ

\circ

\times

\times

\circ

\underset{}{\alpha}

\circ

\circ

\circ

\times

\circ

\circ

\circ

\underset{}{\beta}

\circ

\circ

\times

\times

\times

\circ

\times

\underset{}{\varinjlim}

\circ

\circ

\times

\circ

\circ

\underset{}{\varprojlim}

\times

\times

\circ

\times

\times

4.2
Ind-sheaves on Bordered Spaces

For the results in this subsection, we refer to
D’Agnolo-Kashiwara
[
9
]
.
A bordered space is a pair
M_{\infty}=(M,\overset{\vee}{M})
of
a good topological space
\overset{\vee}{M}
and
an open subset
M\subset\overset{\vee}{M}
.
A morphism
f:(M,\overset{\vee}{M})\to(N,\overset{\vee}{N})
of bordered spaces
is a continuous map
f:M\to N
such that the first projection

\overset{\vee}{M}\times\overset{\vee}{N}\to\overset{\vee}{M}
is proper on
the closure
\overline{\Gamma}_{f}
of the graph
\Gamma_{f}
of
f

in
\overset{\vee}{M}\times\overset{\vee}{N}
.
If also the second projection
\overline{\Gamma}_{f}\to\overset{\vee}{N}
is proper,
we say that
f
is semi-proper.
The category of good topological spaces embeds into that
of bordered spaces by the identification
M=(M,M)
.
We define the triangulated category of ind-sheaves on

M_{\infty}=(M,\overset{\vee}{M})
by

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}}):={\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{\overset{\vee}{M}})/{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{\overset{\vee}{M}\backslash M}).

(4.2)

The quotient functor

\mathbf{q}:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{\overset{\vee}{M}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})

(4.3)

has a left adjoint
\mathbf{l}
and a right
adjoint
\mathbf{r}
, both fully faithful, defined by

\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{q}F):=\mathbb{C}_{M}\otimes F,\hskip 25.0pt\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{q}F):={\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\mathbb{C}_{M},F).

(4.4)

For a morphism
f:M_{\infty}\to N_{\infty}

of bordered spaces, we have
the Grothendieck’s operations

\displaystyle\otimes

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}f_{\ast}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N_{\infty}}),

\displaystyle f^{-1}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}f_{!!}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N_{\infty}}),

\displaystyle f^{!}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{N_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}})

(see
[
9
, Definitions 3.3.1 and 3.3.4]
for the details).
Moreover, there exists a natural embedding of categories

\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M_{\infty}}).}

(4.5)

4.3
Enhanced Sheaves

For the results in this subsection, see
Kashiwara-Schapira
[
32
]
and
D’Agnolo-Kashiwara
[
10
]
.
Let
M
be a good topological space.
We consider the maps

M\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\xrightarrow{p_{1},p_{2},\mu}M\times\mathbb{R}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}M

(4.6)

where
p_{1},p_{2}
are the first and the second projections
and we set
\pi(x,t):=x
and

\mu(x,t_{1},t_{2}):=(x,t_{1}+t_{2})
.
Then the convolution functors for
sheaves on
M\times\mathbb{R}
are defined by

\displaystyle F_{1}\overset{+}{\otimes}F_{2}

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\mu_{!}(p_{1}^{-1}F_{1}\otimes p_{2}^{-1}F_{2}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{+}(F_{1},F_{2})

\displaystyle:={\rm R}p_{1\ast}{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om(p_{2}^{-1}F_{1},\mu^{!}F_{2}).

We define the triangulated category of enhanced sheaves on
M
by

{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M}):={\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M\times\mathbb{R}})/\pi^{-1}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M}).

(4.7)

Then the quotient functor

\mathbf{Q}:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M\times\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})

(4.8)

has fully faithful left and right adjoints

\mathbf{L}^{\rm E},\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}
defined by

\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}(\mathbf{Q}F):=(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{\{t\leq 0\}})\overset{+}{\otimes}F,\hskip 20.0pt\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}(\mathbf{Q}G):={\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{+}(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{\{t\leq 0\}},G),

(4.9)

where
\{t\geq 0\}
stands for
\{(x,t)\in M\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ t\geq 0\}
and
\{t\leq 0\}
is
defined similarly. The convolution functors
are defined also for enhanced sheaves. We denote them
by the same symbols
\overset{+}{\otimes}
,
{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{+}
.
For a continuous map
f:M\to N
, we
can define naturally the operations

\mathbf{E}f^{-1}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{\ast}
,
\mathbf{E}f^{!}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{!}

for enhanced sheaves.
We have also a
natural embedding
\varepsilon:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})
defined by

\varepsilon(F):=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\otimes\pi^{-1}F).

(4.10)

For a continuous function
\varphi:U\to\mathbb{R}

defined on an open subset
U\subset M
of
M
we define
the exponential enhanced sheaf by

{\rm E}_{U|M}^{\varphi}:=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C}_{\{t+\varphi\geq 0\}}),

(4.11)

where
\{t+\varphi\geq 0\}
stands for

\{(x,t)\in M\times{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ x\in U,t+\varphi(x)\geq 0\}
.

4.4
Enhanced Ind-sheaves

We recall some basic notions
and results on enhanced ind-sheaves. References are made to
D’Agnolo-Kashiwara
[
9
]
and
Kashiwara-Schapira
[
33
]
.
Let
M
be a good topological space.
Set
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}:=(\mathbb{R},\overline{\mathbb{R}})
for

\overline{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathbb{R}\sqcup\{-\infty,+\infty\}
,
and let
t\in\mathbb{R}
be the affine coordinate.
We consider the maps

M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}^{2}\xrightarrow{p_{1},p_{2},\mu}M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}M

(4.12)

where
p_{1},p_{2}
and
\pi
are morphisms
of bordered spaces induced by the projections.
And
\mu
is a morphism of bordered spaces induced by the map

M\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(x,t_{1}+t_{2})\in M\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then the convolution functors for
ind-sheaves on
M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
are defined by

\displaystyle F_{1}\overset{+}{\otimes}F_{2}

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\mu_{!!}(p_{1}^{-1}F_{1}\otimes p_{2}^{-1}F_{2}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}(F_{1},F_{2})

\displaystyle:={\rm R}p_{1\ast}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(p_{2}^{-1}F_{1},\mu^{!}F_{2}).

Now we define the triangulated category
of enhanced ind-sheaves on
M
by

{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}):={\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}})/\pi^{-1}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}).

(4.13)

Note that we have a natural embedding of categories

{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})\xhookrightarrow{\ \ \ }{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}).

(4.14)

The quotient functor

\mathbf{Q}:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}})\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})

(4.15)

has fully faithful left and right adjoints

\mathbf{L}^{\rm E},\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}
defined by

\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}(\mathbf{Q}K):=(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{\{t\leq 0\}})\overset{+}{\otimes}K,\hskip 20.0pt\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}(\mathbf{Q}K):={\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\oplus\mathbb{C}_{\{t\leq 0\}},K),

(4.16)

where
\{t\geq 0\}
stands for

\{(x,t)\in M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ t\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0\}

and
\{t\leq 0\}
is defined similarly.

The convolution functors
are defined also for enhanced ind-sheaves. We denote them
by the same symbols
\overset{+}{\otimes}
,
{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}
.
For a continuous map
f:M\to N
, we
can define also the operations

\mathbf{E}f^{-1}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{\ast}
,
\mathbf{E}f^{!}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{!!}

for enhanced ind-sheaves. For example,
by the natural morphism
\widetilde{f}:M\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}\to N\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
of bordered spaces associated to

f
we set
\mathbf{E}f_{\ast}(\mathbf{Q}K)=\mathbf{Q}({\rm R}\widetilde{f}_{\ast}(K))
.
The other operations are defined similarly.
We thus obtain the six operations
\overset{+}{\otimes}
,
{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}
,

\mathbf{E}f^{-1}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{\ast}
,
\mathbf{E}f^{!}
,
\mathbf{E}f_{!!}

for enhanced ind-sheaves .
Moreover we denote by
{\rm D}_{M}^{\rm E}

the Verdier duality functor for enhanced ind-sheaves.
We have outer hom functors

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{\rm E}(K_{1},K_{2})

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\pi_{\ast}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}K_{1},\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}K_{2})\simeq{\rm R}\pi_{\ast}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}K_{1},\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}K_{2}),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{\rm E}(K_{1},K_{2})

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{M}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{\rm E}(K_{1},K_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHom}^{\rm E}(K_{1},K_{2})

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\Gamma(M;{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{\rm E}(K_{1},K_{2})),

with values in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M}),{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{M})
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C})
, respectively.
Moreover for
F\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})
and
K\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})

the objects

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}F\otimes K

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{Q}(\pi^{-1}F\otimes\mathbf{L}^{\rm E}K),

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}F,K)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{Q}\big({\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}F,\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}K)\big).

in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})
are well-defined.
Set
\mathbb{C}_{M}^{\rm E}:=\mathbf{Q}\Bigl(``\underset{a\to+\infty}{\varinjlim}"\ \mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq a\}}\Bigr)\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})
.
Then we have
natural embeddings
\varepsilon,e:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{M})
defined by

\displaystyle\varepsilon(F)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbb{C}_{\{t\geq 0\}}\otimes\pi^{-1}F)

\displaystyle e(F)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{C}_{M}^{\rm E}\otimes\pi^{-1}F\simeq\mathbb{C}_{M}^{\rm E}\overset{+}{\otimes}\varepsilon(F).

For a continuous function
\varphi:U\to\mathbb{R}

defined on an open subset
U\subset M
of
M
we define
the exponential enhanced ind-sheaf by

\mathbb{E}_{U|M}^{\varphi}:=\mathbb{C}_{M}^{\rm E}\overset{+}{\otimes}{\rm E}_{U|M}^{\varphi}=\mathbb{C}_{M}^{\rm E}\overset{+}{\otimes}\mathbf{Q}\mathbb{C}_{\{t+\varphi\geq 0\}}=\mathbf{Q}\Bigl(``\underset{a\to+\infty}{\varinjlim}"\ \mathbb{C}_{\{t+\varphi\geq a\}}\Bigr)

(4.17)

where
\{t+\varphi\geq 0\}
stands for

\{(x,t)\in M\times\overline{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ t\in\mathbb{R},x\in U,t+\varphi(x)\geq 0\}
.

4.5
D-modules

In this subsection we recall some basic notions
and results on D-modules.
References are made to

[
21
]
,
[
29
, §7]
,

[
9
, §8, 9]
,

[
33
, §3, 4, 7]
and

[
25
, §4, 5, 6, 7, 8]
.
For a complex manifold
X
we denote by

d_{X}
its complex dimension.
Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{X},\Omega_{X}
and
\mathcal{D}_{X}

the sheaves of holomorphic functions,
holomorphic differential forms of top degree
and holomorphic differential operators, respectively.
Let
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
be the bounded derived category
of left
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}_{X})

be that of right
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-modules.
Moreover we denote by
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
,
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm good}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
,

{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize hol}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
the full triangulated subcategories
of
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
consisting of objects with coherent, good,
holonomic and regular holonomic cohomologies,
respectively.
For a morphism
f:X\to Y
of complex manifolds,
denote by
\overset{D}{\otimes},{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om_{\mathcal{D}_{X}},\mathbf{D}f_{\ast},\mathbf{D}f^{\ast}

the standard operations for D-modules.
We define also the duality functor
\mathbb{D}_{X}:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

by

\mathbb{D}_{X}(\mathcal{M}):={\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{D}_{X})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\otimes-1}_{X}[d_{X}].

(4.18)

Note that there exists
an equivalence of categories

(\cdot)^{\rm r}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Mod}(\mathcal{D}^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}_{X})
given by

\mathcal{M}^{\rm r}:=\Omega_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{M}.

(4.19)

The classical de Rham and solution functors are defined by

\displaystyle DR_{X}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 40.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto\Omega_{X}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{M},

\displaystyle Sol_{X}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 30.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Then for
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

we have an isomorphism
Sol_{X}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}]\simeq DR_{X}(\mathbb{D}_{X}\mathcal{M})
.
For a closed hypersurface
D\subset X

in
X
we denote by
\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)

the sheaf of meromorphic functions on
X
with poles in
D
.
Then for
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
we set

\mathcal{M}(\ast D):=\mathcal{M}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D).

(4.20)

For
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)
and
U:=X\backslash D
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{X}e^{f}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{X}/\{P\in\mathcal{D}_{X}\ |\ Pe^{f}|_{U}=0\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{U|X}^{f}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{X}e^{f}(\ast D).

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{U|X}^{f}
is holonomic and there exists an isomorphism

\mathbb{D}_{X}(\mathcal{E}_{U|X}^{f})(\ast D)\simeq\mathcal{E}_{U|X}^{-f}.

(4.21)

Namely
\mathcal{E}_{U|X}^{f}
is a meromorphic connection associated
to
d+df
.

One defines the ind-sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm t}
of tempered
holomorphic functions
as the Dolbeault complex with coefficients in the ind-sheaf
of tempered distributions.
More precisely, denoting by
\overline{X}
the complex conjugate manifold
to
X
and by
X_{\mathbb{R}}
the underlying real analytic manifold of
X
,
we set

\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm t}:={\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\overline{X}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}},\mathcal{D}b_{X_{\mathbb{R}}}^{\rm t}),

(4.22)

where
\mathcal{D}b_{X_{\mathbb{R}}}^{\rm t}
is the ind-sheaf of
tempered distributions on
X_{\mathbb{R}}

(for the definition see
[
29
, Definition 7.2.5]
).
Moreover, we set

\Omega_{X}^{\rm t}:=\beta_{X}\Omega_{X}\otimes_{\beta_{X}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm t}.

(4.23)

Then the tempered de Rham and solution functors
are defined by

\displaystyle DR_{X}^{\rm t}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 40.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto\Omega_{X}^{\rm t}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{M},

\displaystyle Sol_{X}^{\rm t}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 40.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm t}).

Note that we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle Sol_{X}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\alpha_{X}Sol_{X}^{\rm t}(\mathcal{M}),

\displaystyle DR_{X}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\alpha_{X}DR_{X}^{\rm t}(\mathcal{M}),

\displaystyle Sol_{X}^{\rm t}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}]

\displaystyle\simeq DR_{X}^{\rm t}(\mathbb{D}_{X}\mathcal{M}).

Let
i:X\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}\to X\times{\mathbb{P}}
be
the natural morphism of bordered spaces and

\tau\in\mathbb{C}\subset{\mathbb{P}}
the affine coordinate
such that
\tau|_{\mathbb{R}}
is that of
\mathbb{R}
.
We then define objects
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathcal{D}_{X})
and

\Omega_{X}^{\rm E}\in{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathcal{D}_{X}^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}

\displaystyle:={\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\overline{X}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}},\mathcal{D}b_{X_{\mathbb{R}}}^{{\rm T}})

\displaystyle\simeq i^{!}\bigl((\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-\tau})^{\rm r}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}}\mathcal{O}_{X\times{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\bigr)[1]\simeq i^{!}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}}(\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{\tau},\mathcal{O}_{X\times{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t})[2],

\displaystyle\Omega_{X}^{\rm E}

\displaystyle:=\Omega_{X}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}\simeq i^{!}(\Omega_{X\times{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-\tau})[1],

where
\mathcal{D}b_{X_{\mathbb{R}}}^{{\rm T}}

stand for the enhanced ind-sheaf
of tempered distributions on
X_{\mathbb{R}}

(for the definition see
[
9
, Definition 8.1.1]
).
We call
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}
the enhanced ind-sheaf
of tempered holomorphic functions.
Note that there exists an isomorphism

i_{0}^{!}\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm t},

(4.24)

where
i_{0}:X\to X\times\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
is the
inclusion map of bordered spaces induced by
x\mapsto(x,0)
.
The enhanced de Rham and solution functors
are defined by

\displaystyle DR_{X}^{\rm E}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 40.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto\Omega_{X}^{\rm E}\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{M},

\displaystyle Sol_{X}^{\rm E}

\displaystyle:{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})^{\mbox{\scriptsize op}}\to{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X}),\hskip 40.0pt\mathcal{M}\longmapsto{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E}).

Then for
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize coh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})

we have isomorphism
Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}]\simeq DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathbb{D}_{X}\mathcal{M})

and

Sol^{{\rm t}}_{X}(\mathcal{M})\simeq i_{0}^{!}{\mathbf{R}^{\rm E}}Sol_{X}^{{\rm E}}(\mathcal{M}).

We recall the following results of
[
9
]
.

Theorem 4.1
.

(i)

For
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
there
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

{\rm D}_{X}^{\rm E}\bigl(DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})\bigr)\simeq Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}].

(4.25)

(ii)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of complex manifolds.
Then for
\mathcal{N}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{Y})

there is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}({\mathbf{D}}f^{\ast}\mathcal{N})\simeq\mathbf{E}f^{-1}Sol_{Y}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{N}).

(4.26)

(iii)

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism of complex manifolds
and
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm good}(\mathcal{D}_{X})\cap{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
.
If
{\rm supp}(\mathcal{M})
is proper over Y then there
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{Y})

Sol_{Y}^{\rm E}({\mathbf{D}}f_{\ast}\mathcal{M})[d_{Y}]\simeq\mathbf{E}f_{\ast}Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}].

(4.27)

(iv)

For
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
,
there exists an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}_{1}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{M}_{2})\simeq Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}_{1})\overset{+}{\otimes}Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}_{2}).

(4.28)

(v)

If
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and
D\subset X

is a closed hypersurface, then there
are isomorphisms in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

\displaystyle Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}(\ast D))

\displaystyle\simeq\pi^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{X\backslash D}\otimes Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}),

\displaystyle DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}(\ast D))

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{X\backslash D},DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})).

(vi)

Let
D
be a closed hypersurface in
X
and

f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)
a meromorphic function along
D
.
Set
U:=X\setminus D\subset X
.
Then there exists an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{f}\big)\simeq\mathbb{E}_{U|X}^{\rm Ref}.

(4.29)

(vii)

For
\mathcal{L}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and

\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
,
there exists an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{L}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{M})\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{L}),DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})).

(4.30)

We also have the following corollary of Theorem
4.1
.

Corollary 4.2
.

For
\mathcal{L}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mbox{\rm\scriptsize rh}}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
and

\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
,
there exists an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{L}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{M})\simeq\pi^{-1}Sol_{X}(\mathcal{L})\otimes Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}).

(4.31)

Proof.

Let

{\rm D}_{X}^{\rm E}(\cdot):{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})^{\rm op}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X}),\qquad F\longmapsto{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}(F,\omega^{\rm E}_{X})

(4.32)

be the Verdier dual functor defined in

[
9
, Section 4.8]
, where
we set
\omega^{\rm E}_{X}=\mathbb{C}_{X}^{\rm E}\otimes\pi^{-1}\omega_{X}
.
Moreover we set
L=Sol_{X}(\mathcal{L})

and
K=Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})
.
Then we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle{\rm D}_{X}^{\rm E}(\pi^{-1}L\otimes K)\simeq

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}L,{\rm D}_{X}^{\rm E}(K))

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom(\pi^{-1}L,DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M})[d_{X}])

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle DR_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{L}\overset{D}{\otimes}\mathcal{M})[d_{X}],

where the first isomorphism follows from
the definition of the bifunctor

{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom^{+}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
in the second (resp. third) isomorphism
we used Theorem
4.1
(i)
(resp. Theorem
4.1
(vii)). By taking
the Verdier dual
{\rm D}_{X}^{\rm E}(\cdot)
of
the both sides, we obtain the desired
isomorphism.
∎

We recall also the following theorem of
[
9
]
.

Theorem 4.3

(
[
9
, Theorem 9.5.3 (Irregular
Riemann-Hilbert Correspondence)]
)
.

There exists an isomorphism functorial
with respect to
\mathcal{M}\in{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X}):

\mathcal{M}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\rm R}{\mathcal{H}}om^{\rm E}(Sol_{X}^{\rm E}(\mathcal{M}),\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\rm E})

(4.33)

in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}_{\rm hol}(\mathcal{D}_{X})
.

For the proof of the main results in
[
9
]
,
a key role was played by the following proposition.
Here we give a very short new proof
deduced directly from
[
30
, Propositon 7.3 and Remark 7.4]

(see also
[
22
, Proposition 3.14]
).

Proposition 4.4

(
[
9
, Proposition 6.2.2]
)
.

Let
D
be a closed hypersurface in
X
and

f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)
a meromorphic function along
D
.
Set
U:=X\setminus D\subset X
.
Then there exists an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})

DR_{X}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{-f}\big)\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{U},\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Ref<a\}}\Big)[d_{X}].

(4.34)

Proof.

Let
z
be the standard holomorphic coordinate of
\mathbb{C}
.
As in
[
9
, Lemma 6.2.5]
, first we consider the
exponetial D-module

\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}
on the one dimensional
projective space
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
associated to
the meromorphic function
f=z\in\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(\ast\{\infty\})
.
Then for the holomorphic coordinate
\zeta:=\frac{1}{z}

on a neighborhood of the point

\infty\in{\mathbb{P}}
there exist isomorphisms

\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{\zeta}\Bigr)\simeq\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}/\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(\zeta^{2}\partial_{\zeta}+1).

(4.35)

This implies that on a neighborhood of
\infty\in{\mathbb{P}}

we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}/\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(-\partial_{\zeta}\zeta^{2}+1)\simeq\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{\zeta^{2}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\zeta}\Bigr)\Bigr\}

(4.36)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{\zeta}\Bigr)\simeq\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}

(4.37)

(cf.
[
9
, Lemma 6.1.2 and Remark 6.1.3]
).
In this case, we thus obtain an isomorphism

DR_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}\big)\simeq Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)[1].

(4.38)

Consider the distinguished triangle

H^{0}Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\longrightarrow Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\longrightarrow\tau^{\geq 1}Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\overset{+1}{\longrightarrow}

(4.39)

in
{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{b}}({\rm I}\mathbb{C}_{X})
. Then, according to

[
30
, Remark 7.4]
(see also
[
22
, Proposition 3.14]
),
for the closed
embedding
i_{\infty}:\{\infty\}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}
there exists an isomorphism

\tau^{\geq 1}Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\simeq(i_{\infty})_{*}\mathbb{C}_{\{\infty\}}[-1].

(4.40)

From this and
i_{\infty}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}}\simeq 0

we obtain the vanishing

{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}},\tau^{\geq 1}Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\Big)\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(i_{\infty}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{C}_{\{\infty\}}[-1]\Big)\simeq 0.

(4.41)

On the other hand, by
[
30
, Proposition 7.3]

(see also
[
22
, Proposition 3.14]
)
we have an isomorphism

H^{0}Sol_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{z}\big)\simeq\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{z\in\mathbb{C}|\rm Rez<a\}}.

(4.42)

Then the assertion follows immediately from
the isomorphisms

DR_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}\big)\simeq DR_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}(\ast\{\infty\})\big)\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\mathbb{C}},DR_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}\big)\Big)

(4.43)

(see
[
29
, Theorem 7.4.12]
) and the
distinguished triangle (
4.39
).
Next, we consider the general exponential D-module

\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{-f}
for
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(\ast D)
.
Let
\nu:Y\longrightarrow X
be a proper
morphism of complex manifolds such that

E:=\nu^{-1}D\subset Y
is a normal crossing
divisor in
Y
, the restriction

\nu|_{Y\setminus E}:Y\setminus E\longrightarrow X\setminus D
of

\nu
is an isomorphism and the meromorphic
function
g:=f\circ\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\ast E)

on
Y
has no point of indeterminacy on the
whole
Y
. Such a resolution of singularities of

D\subset X
always exists. See for example
the proof of
[
45
, Theorem 3.6]
.
Let
E_{0}
(resp.
E_{1}
) be the union of
the irreducible components of
E
along
which
g
has no pole (resp. has a pole)
so that we have
E=E_{0}\cup E_{1}
. Set

V:=Y\setminus E
and
\tilde{V}:=Y\setminus E_{1}\supset V
. Then
g\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\ast E)
extends to a holomorphic
function on
\tilde{V}
and we obtain a
meromorphic function

\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\ast E_{1})
. Moreover
there exists an isomorphism

\mathscr{E}_{V|Y}^{-g}\simeq\mathscr{E}_{\tilde{V}|Y}^{-\tilde{g}}(\ast E_{0}).

(4.44)

Since we have an isomorphism

\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{-f}\simeq\Big({\mathbf{D}}\nu_{\ast}\mathscr{E}_{V|Y}^{-g}\Big)(\ast D),

(4.45)

by
[
29
, Theorems 7.4.6 and 7.4.12]

we obtain an isomorphism

DR_{X}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{-f}\big)\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{V},DR_{Y}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\tilde{V}|Y}^{-\tilde{g}}\big)\Big).

(4.46)

Since the meromorphic function

\tilde{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\ast E_{1})
has no point
of indeterminacy on the whole
Y
,
we obtain a holomorphic map from
Y
to

{\mathbb{P}}
. We denote it by the same letter
\tilde{g}

for simplicity. Then by

[
29
, Theorem 7.4.1]
it follows from
the isomorphism

\mathscr{E}_{\tilde{V}|Y}^{-\tilde{g}}\simeq{\mathbf{D}}\tilde{g}^{*}\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}

(4.47)

that we obtain an isomorphism

DR_{Y}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\tilde{V}|Y}^{-\tilde{g}}\big)\simeq\tilde{g}^{!}DR_{{\mathbb{P}}}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{C}|{\mathbb{P}}}^{-z}\big)[1-d_{X}]\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\tilde{V}},\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \tilde{g}^{!}\mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Rez<a\}}\Big)[2-d_{X}].

(4.48)

Since for an open neighborhood
W
of the normal
crossing divisor
E_{1}=\tilde{g}^{-1}(\infty)\subset Y
the restriction of the morphism

\tilde{g}:Y\longrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}
to

\tilde{V}\cap W
is a topological submersion,
we have an isomorphism

\tilde{g}^{!}\mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Rez<a\}}\simeq\tilde{g}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Rez<a\}}[2d_{X}-2]

on
\tilde{V}\cap W
. Moreover, if
a>0
is
large enough, on
\tilde{V}\setminus W

we have

\tilde{g}^{!}\mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Rez<a\}}\simeq\tilde{g}^{!}\mathbb{C}_{{\mathbb{P}}}\simeq\tilde{g}^{!}\omega_{{\mathbb{P}}}[-2]\simeq\omega_{Y}[-2]\simeq\mathbb{C}_{Y}[2d_{X}-2]\simeq\tilde{g}^{-1}\mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Rez<a\}}[2d_{X}-2].

(4.49)

Combining these results together, we finally
get the desired isomorphism as follows:

\displaystyle DR_{X}^{\rm t}\big(\mathscr{E}_{U|X}^{-f}\big)\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{V},{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{\tilde{V}},\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Re\tilde{g}<a\}}\Big)[d_{X}]\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}\nu_{*}{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{V},\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Reg<a\}}\Big)[d_{X}]

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{U},{\rm R}\nu_{*}\Big(\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Reg<a\}}\Big)\Big)[d_{X}]

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm R}{\mathcal{I}}hom\Big(\mathbb{C}_{U},\underset{a\to+\infty}{``\varinjlim"}\ \mathbb{C}_{\{\rm Ref<a\}}\Big)[d_{X}].

This completes the proof.
∎

5
Several Micro-supports Related to
Holonomic

D-modules

In this section, we introduce several micro-supports
